AI: **1. One-Time Repository Setup (Step-by-Step)**

*Initial local setup:*
- Download the starter zip from Moodle, unzip it (→ `doc-agent-starter` folder)
- Create a **NEW, EMPTY** GitHub repository — do **NOT** add a README or .gitignore (the folder already has them)
- Name it `doc-agent-<your team id>` (e.g. `doc-agent-G07`)
- Make it **Public — REQUIRED** (a private repo cannot be graded and counts as no submission)
- Turn the unzipped folder into that repo and push:
```
git init
git add .
git commit -m "A1 start"
git branch -M main
git remote add origin <your repo URL>
git push -u origin main
```
- **Order matters:** the empty public repo must exist on github.com *first*, because `git push` can only push to a repo that already exists there. `git init`, `git add`, and `git commit` are local-only and never touch GitHub — only `git push` uploads.

*Authentication/credentials:*
- If `git push` asks for a password and fails, GitHub no longer accepts account passwords over HTTPS. Use one of:
  - A Personal Access Token as the password (github.com → Settings → Developer settings → Personal access tokens, with **repo** scope)
  - GitHub Desktop
  - SSH keys

*Team setup:*
- Add your two teammates as collaborators (Settings → Collaborators)
- Send your instructor the repository URL — this is your team's submission address for **all four milestones** (same repo reused)

**2. Daily Team Workflow & Anti-Patterns**

*Daily rules:*
- Each member commits under their **own** GitHub account (contribution is tracked per person)
- Commit little and often
- Put your filled assignment form, your `grading_kit/`, and your code in the repo
- Every file already has a named stub — you **edit** it, you do **not** create new files
- Do **NOT** commit your full corpus of page-images (it's large) — `scripts/get_data.sh` fetches it instead; commit only the few held-out sample pages under `grading_kit/heldout_pages/`

*Anti-patterns / red flags:*
- Work committed only by one account "looks like one person did everything"
- A commit history that "appears all at once the night before a deadline is a red flag and gets reviewed by a human" (a steady, weeks-long history "looks like real work")
- Creating new files instead of editing the provided stubs (implied violation, though the document doesn't state an explicit penalty for this — flagging that as not spelled out)
- Committing the full corpus of page-images

**3. Milestone Submission Protocol**

Exact commands (using A1 as the example given; substitute A2/A3/A4 and the matching tag):
```
git add -A
git commit -m "A1 submission"      # use A1 / A2 / A3 / A4
git tag a1-submit                  # use a1-submit / a2-submit / a3-submit / a4-submit
git push origin main --tags
```
- `git push origin main --tags` **is** the submission.
- The tag `a1-submit` (or `a2-submit`, etc.) "permanently marks your repo exactly as it was at the deadline" — you may keep working afterward without altering that marked snapshot.

*On timestamps/deadlines:*
- Deadlines are read from **GitHub's servers**, not your computer's clock.
- Changing a commit's date locally does nothing — what counts is when your push **reaches GitHub**.
- Explicit instruction: push before the deadline.

**4. Pre-Submission Checklist**

Before pushing a submission tag, the document lists exactly:
- ☐ The milestone's form is filled in
- ☐ `grading_kit/manifest.yaml` declares your axes — domain, data-speciality, NFR + target — and points to the corpus
- ☐ Each member has added their `transcripts/<student number>.txt`
- ☐ The code for this milestone runs (`make test` passes what it should)
- ☐ You created and pushed the correct tag: `aN-submit`

**What we read at each milestone** (stated in the doc, relevant context for the checklist):
- Your repository at the tag `aN-submit` (code + form + `grading_kit/`)
- The GitHub commit timeline (when work happened; who did what)
- What changed since your previous milestone
- Your per-member AI-chat transcripts under `transcripts/<your student number>.txt`

AI: # doc-agent — Architecture Briefing (Lead Architect View)

## 1. The Core Architecture & Seams (Mental Model)

**Fixed vs. fill-in — the basic contract:**
Think of the repo as a house with load-bearing walls already built. You furnish the rooms; you don't move the walls.

- **FIXED (never touch structurally):** `contracts.py` (data shapes — `Page`, `Region`, `Chunk`, `Query`, `Citation`, `Answer`, `ToolResult`), `hooks.py` (the seam mechanism itself), `pipeline.py` (the Stage 0–9 order), the agent loop in `agent.py`, and the tool names/signatures in `tools.py`. CI enforces this via `tests/test_structure.py`.
- **What you actually do:** fill in the marked `# IMPLEMENT` function bodies (`[fill]`), write config/tasks/reports (`[author]`), and — for cross-cutting stuff — wire your logic into a fixed attachment point (`[wire]`).
- **Where you make choices:** only in `configs/` — which model, which parameters, per stage. You never choose *where* code lives.

**Cross-cutting features (grounding, security, PII, tracing) — how they actually work:**
These aren't single files you edit once — they're behaviors that need to fire at multiple points across the whole pipeline (e.g., PII redaction has to happen wherever text could leak; logging has to happen at every step). So instead of scattering that logic everywhere, the architecture uses a **publish-subscribe pattern**:

1. **`hooks.py`** defines named **seams** — fixed checkpoints in the pipeline/agent loop (e.g., `BEFORE_ANSWER`, `ON_STEP`, `ON_TOOL_CALL`, `AFTER_ANSWER`).
2. Each feature (e.g., `llm/postprocess.py` for grounding, `governance/pii.py` for redaction, `logging_conf.py` for tracing) owns its logic and calls `register(seam, handler)` to attach itself to a seam.
3. **`wiring.py` → `register_all(cfg)`** is the *single manifest* that actually turns all these registrations on — one place to audit "what's wired where."
4. At runtime, `hooks.run(seam, ctx)` fires every handler registered at that seam, in order, threading context through.

So: **owner module has the logic, `hooks.py` defines when it runs, `wiring.py` is the master switch-list.** `docs/cross-cutting.md` documents the full owner→touchpoint map.

**The "Strict No-File-Creation" rule:**
Every deliverable — every Python module, notebook, script, report, config, test — **already exists as a named stub in the starter repo.** Your job across all four milestones is to *edit those stubs*, never create new files. This applies uniformly: from `src/doc_agent/*` implementation files down to `reports/final_report.md` and even the optional-feature backlog (E1–E39) — each enhancement has a pre-assigned home file. If you're creating a new file, you're off-spec.

---

## 2. Milestone Execution Roadmap (A1 → A4)

**A1 — Framing & Data Setup**
Lock in your project identity and get usable data flowing:
- Declare your three axes (domain, data speciality, NFR+target) in `task.yaml`
- Pick per-stage models in `config.yaml`
- Fetch your corpus (`get_data.sh`), document its provenance/licence
- Declare axes + corpus pointers in `grading_kit/manifest.yaml`; seed `heldout_pages/` + `labels.jsonl`
- Enforce data quality gates (`validate.py` — min 300 pages/60k words, split by document) and version the corpus (`versioning.py`)
- Explore the data in `eda.ipynb`; get logging and secrets management running
*Net result: a scoped project, a validated corpus, and infrastructure plumbing in place.*

**A2 — Knowledge Base Build (Ingestion → Vision/OCR → Indexing)**
Turn raw scanned pages into a searchable index — this is the vision pipeline:
- Load pages → preprocess (deskew/denoise/binarize) → detect layout regions → OCR/transcribe them
- Chunk the extracted text → embed it → build/load the vector store
- Prove it works: `kb_demo.ipynb` (OCR quality + one retrieval example), `pipeline_diagram.md`, and the design-choices table
- **Plus:** implement *only* your one data-speciality enhancement (e.g., degraded scans, multi-column layout, HTR) and *only* your one indexing-related NFR enhancement, if applicable
*Net result: a working, queryable knowledge base built from your specific corpus.*

**A3 — Agent, Retrieval & Evaluation**
Build the actual reasoning system and prove it's grounded:
- Wire real top-k retrieval; implement the LLM call wrapper and prompts
- Implement every agent tool (retrieve, rerank, read_page, enhance_page, extract, aggregate, cite, calculator, escalate_to_human)
- Implement `decide()`, `act()`, `synthesize()` in the agent loop — critically, **`decide()` must implement evidence-gated re-search**: if the top retrieval score is weak, widen `k` and retry; abstain once `k` exceeds `k_max`. This is graded fail-closed by the A3 agentic gate.
- Wire grounding/abstention into `BEFORE_ANSWER`, wire tracing into `ON_STEP`/`ON_TOOL_CALL`/`AFTER_ANSWER`, emit `traces/run.jsonl`
- Train any fine-tuned components; compute eval metrics (OCR-F1, recall@k, groundedness, citation accuracy, subgroup gap) against `labels.jsonl`
- Run an ablation study; author `tasks.jsonl` + `success_check.py` **including at least one `needs_research:true/false` task pair** so the agentic gate can verify re-search actually fires
- Reproduce everything via `run.sh`, write `eval_report.md`, keep tests + CI green
- **Plus:** implement only your one NFR enhancement (reranking, hybrid retrieval, calibration, explainability, fairness, robustness, security, privacy, auditability, etc. — pick the one matching your declared NFR)
*Net result: a working, evidence-gated, evaluated agent with proof it's grounded and not hallucinating.*

**A4 — Serving, MLOps & Governance**
Ship it and account for it:
- Serve `/answer` over an API; launch the Gradio UI/demo
- Wire experiment tracking (MLOps backbone)
- Write the model card/datasheet, the final report, the one-page brochure, and demo/video links
- Un-skip the end-to-end smoke test — it must return a grounded, cited answer, CI green
- **Plus:** implement only your one serving/governance-related NFR enhancement (offline/portable, low-latency, cost-efficient, accessible, or registry/drift-monitoring)
*Net result: a deployed, documented, monitorable system with a governance paper trail.*

---

## 3. Tier System & Rules of Engagement

**How the three tiers interact:**
- **⭐ Core** — every single group builds these, in the stated build order, no exceptions. This is the shared substrate.
- **▲ Gated** — you build *only the one item* that matches your declared data speciality, and *only the one item* that matches your declared primary NFR. You do not attempt every gated item — just your one-per-axis match. These appear after the core list in each milestone, still in stage order.
- **➕ Bonus** — extra credit, attempt only after core (and your gated items) are solid. Never required.

**Practical sequencing rule:** within any milestone, always finish ⭐ Core first, then your one ▲ data-speciality item, then your one ▲ NFR item — in that order. Don't jump to bonus work with an incomplete core.

**Explicit "Never Edit" boundaries (CI will fail you):**
- The agent loop in `agent/agent.py`
- The stage order in `pipeline.py`
- The seams in `hooks.py`
- The data types/contracts in `contracts.py`
- The tool names/signatures in `agent/tools.py`

These five are structurally locked across the entire project — you implement *inside* them (e.g., fill `decide()`'s body), but you never change their shape, order, or interface.

AI: # A2 Completion Guide — "Build Your Knowledge Base"

## 1. Submission Overview & Critical Pitfalls

**What ships, two ways:**
- **GitHub (the real submission):** `git add -A && git commit -m "a2-submit" && git tag a2-submit && git push origin main --tags`. Per the form: "The tag IS your submission (deadlines are read from GitHub's servers) — do NOT upload a ZIP."
- **The ZIP structure** (autograder-enforced, exact match required) contains: `A2_form.docx`, `src/doc_agent/` (ingest, vision, index implemented), `scripts/build_index.sh`, `notebooks/kb_demo.ipynb`, `reports/pipeline_diagram.md`, `configs/design_choices.md`, and `transcripts/<student number>.txt` ×3.

- `data/provenance.md` plus your corpus (or `scripts/get_data.sh`)
- `notebooks/eda.ipynb`
- `grading_kit/manifest.yaml`
- `grading_kit/heldout_pages/` with `labels.jsonl`

**Don't let these slip through the cracks** — even though they were technically "done" in A1, this is the first time they're actually assessed.

**The Grounding Gate — what zeroes you out:**
Stated explicitly: "every claim in the form must match your own code, index, and notebook outputs. Numbers with no notebook evidence score zero for that item." Practical implication: never write a number (OCR error rate, chunk count, embedding dimension) into the form unless it's actually printed/visible in `kb_demo.ipynb` or your code output. No estimates, no round guesses.

## 3. Team Division & AI Transparency Strategy (Section 8)

**Ownership split:**
- Every section must have a named owner in the "Worked sections" column of the Team Members table at the top of the form.
- Each member is responsible for submitting the AI-chat transcript behind **only the sections they led** — not the whole form.
- Given 8 sections (+ the submission mechanics) across 3 people, a natural split is roughly 2–3 sections per person, but the form doesn't mandate a specific division — just that "every section must be owned by someone."

**Transcript file requirements — exact format:**
- Filename: exactly `<student number>.txt` (plain text), one per member, three total.
- Location: `transcripts/` folder (per the ZIP structure table).
- Required contents, **in this order**:
  1. **Header** — member name, student number, sections led
  2. **The full conversation** — every prompt and every AI reply, **unedited**
  3. **A short reflection** — what was accepted, changed, or rejected from the AI's output, and why

Me: as the naming is not consistent, how will we generalize this for 224 issues?

AI: Good instinct to ask this now, before you write the script — this is exactly the design fork that determines whether Day 1's script actually satisfies the "works for 10–15 now, extend to 224 later" requirement.

**The key insight: you don't need to guess or hardcode all 224 identifiers manually.** Internet Archive has a **collection-level search/metadata API** that lets you programmatically ask "give me every identifier in the `byte-magazine` collection" — regardless of how inconsistently each one is named. This solves your generalization problem entirely.

## Two ways to do this

**Option A — the `ia` command-line tool (recommended, simplest)**
Internet Archive publishes an official CLI (`pip install internetarchive`) that can search and list a whole collection:
```bash
ia search 'collection:byte-magazine' --itemlist
```
This returns **every identifier** in the collection, one per line, regardless of naming pattern. You don't need to know the format in advance — IA's own indexing handles that.

**Option B — the Advanced Search API directly (no extra tool)**
```bash
curl "https://archive.org/advancedsearch.php?q=collection:byte-magazine&fl[]=identifier&rows=300&output=json"
```
This returns a JSON list of identifiers you can parse with `jq` or Python.

## How this reshapes your script's design

Instead of hardcoding an array, `get_data.sh` should:
1. **Fetch the full identifier list** from the collection via `ia search` or the API (this works identically whether you want 10 issues or 224)
2. **Select a subset** — for now, filter/slice to your chosen 10–15 (e.g., by year, or just `head -15`)
3. **Download only that subset** using `ia download <identifier>`
4. Leave a flag or variable (e.g., `MODE=subset` vs `MODE=full`) so switching to the full 224 later is a one-line change, not a rewrite

This is actually a cleaner design than what we discussed before — it directly satisfies your Day 1 requirement ("build it to also work for the full 224 later") because the *fetching mechanism* never needs per-issue hardcoding. The only thing that changes between subset and full-run is which identifiers you pass to `ia download`.

**Your immediate next action:** do you have Python/pip available in your dev environment, so we can `pip install internetarchive` and test `ia search` right now? Or would you rather I show you the pure `curl` + `jq` version so you're not dependent on installing anything extra?

Me: which option is better for my project? also note that my laptop has storage issues

AI: Given your storage constraint, this is actually a very relevant question — let me break down what each option costs you, both now and later.

## Storage impact (the part that matters most for you)

**Neither option downloads any page images by itself** — both `ia search` and the `curl` API call only return a small JSON/text list of identifiers (a few KB total for all 224). The actual storage risk isn't in *this* step, it's in the **next** step when you run `ia download <identifier>` — full BYTE issues can be hundreds of MB to a few GB each in raw scan form (your A1 form itself estimated ~200+ GB for the full 224-issue corpus).

So regardless of which option you pick now, your real storage decision is coming up next: **you must download only specific file formats per issue** (e.g., just the OCR'd text layer or a compressed PDF, not the full raw JP2 image set) or you'll blow through your disk on even 10–15 issues. I'll flag that clearly when we get there.

## Comparing the two options for your actual situation

| | **Option A: `ia` CLI** | **Option B: `curl` + `jq`** |
|---|---|---|
| Install footprint | `pip install internetarchive` — small Python package, negligible disk cost | Nothing to install if `curl`/`jq` already on your system (usually pre-installed on Linux/Mac) |
| Ease of download step later | `ia download <id> --glob="*.pdf"` lets you filter to specific file types per issue — **directly controls storage** | You'd need to separately query each item's file list via the API, then `curl` only the specific files — more manual work |
| Reproducibility for teammates | Anisa/Sunanda just need `pip install internetarchive` too — one shared dependency | No install needed, but more custom script logic to maintain and explain |
| Fits your "generalize to 224 later" need | Built-in `search` + `download` work identically at any scale | Also works at scale, but you're hand-rolling more of the logic |

## My recommendation: **Option A (`ia` CLI)**

The deciding factor for your storage problem specifically: `ia download` has a **built-in `--glob` flag** that lets you grab *only* the file types you actually need (e.g., just the derived text/PDF, skipping the raw multi-GB JP2 image sets) — directly at download time, not as an afterthought. That's the safest way to keep your local `data/raw/` small while still building a script that scales cleanly to 224 issues later.

This flag overrides that protection for a one-off CLI tool like this. AI: Found the real problem — this is a classic PATH mismatch, not just an old pip.

**What's happening:** your `pip` command is resolving to a **Python 2.7** install bundled inside an old Anaconda2 environment (`/home/debashmita/anaconda2/...`), while your actual `python3` is a separate, much newer 3.12.3 install. These are two completely disconnected environments — installing anything with that `pip` would install it into Python 2.7's site-packages, which `python3` (and thus `ia`) would never see anyway. Python 2.7 is also long past end-of-life, so nothing modern is designed to work with it.

**The fix — bypass `pip` entirely and call pip through python3 directly:**

```bash
python3 -m pip --version
```

Paste that output first — this confirms whether `python3` has its own pip module available at all (most 3.12 installs do, but let's verify before installing anything).

**If that shows a valid pip version, install with:**
```bash
python3 -m pip install internetarchive --break-system-packages
```

Using `python3 -m pip` instead of bare `pip` guarantees the package lands in the Python 3.12 environment that `python3` (and the `ia` command it creates) will actually use — sidestepping the Anaconda2 conflict entirely.

AI: Exactly what I flagged — the install succeeded, but `ia` landed in `/home/debashmita/.local/bin`, which isn't on your shell's `PATH`. (Ignore that `sudo apt install internetarchive` suggestion — that would install a different, likely outdated system package, not the one we just set up. Don't run it.)

## Fix: add `~/.local/bin` to your PATH

**1. Check your shell first** (so we edit the right config file):
```bash
echo $SHELL
```

**2. Add the PATH export.** If it says `/bin/bash`, run:
```bash
echo 'export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"' >> ~/.bashrc
source ~/.bashrc
```
If it says `/bin/zsh`, use `~/.zshrc` instead of `~/.bashrc` in both commands.

**Why this works:** `.bashrc` (or `.zshrc`) runs every time you open a new terminal — this line tells your shell to also look inside `~/.local/bin` when resolving commands. `source`-ing it reloads your current terminal session immediately, so you don't have to close and reopen it.

**3. Verify:**
```bash
ia --version
```

Paste me the output — once this shows a real version number, we're finally ready to run your first collection search.

Me: ia --version
5.11.0

AI: `ia` is live and working — version 5.11.0 confirmed. Great, this was worth getting right since your whole team (and future scripting) depends on it.

## Now let's run your first real search against the BYTE collection

```bash
ia search 'collection:byte-magazine' --itemlist
```

**What this does:** queries Internet Archive's metadata index for every item tagged under the `byte-magazine` collection and prints just their identifiers, one per line — no downloading happens here, so this is completely safe for your storage situation.

**A couple of things to expect:**
- This should print somewhere around 224 lines (matching your A1 form's stated corpus size) — a good sanity check that you've got the right collection name.
- It might take a few seconds since it's querying IA's servers live.
- Some `ia` commands require you to be logged in for certain operations (like downloading restricted items), but public metadata search like this one typically works anonymously without any login setup.

**Run it and paste me:**
1. The first ~10 lines of output (so we can see the actual identifier formats at scale — confirming what we saw manually)
2. The total line count, which you can get by running:
```bash
ia search 'collection:byte-magazine' --itemlist | wc -l
```

This total count is actually useful data for your `data/provenance.md` too — it's a documented, reproducible way to state your corpus size instead of an estimate.

AI: This is a really useful (and important) result — it surfaces a discrepancy you need to deal with before going further.

## Key findings from this list

**1. 353 items ≠ your A1 form's stated 224.** Your A1 form said "224 English-language issue entries." The actual collection has 353 items — meaning it includes things beyond your original scope. Looking at the list, several categories don't belong in your corpus:

- **`byteme*` series** (byteme1994_02 through byteme1998_05) — this looks like a *different* publication ("Byte Me"?), not BYTE Magazine itself
- **`byte-romania-*` series** — a Romanian-language regional edition, not English
- **`BYTEListings*` series** (BYTEListingsAprJun1987, etc.) — these are code-listing supplements, not full magazine issues
- **`best_of_byte_volume_1_1977_06`** — a compilation/anthology, not an original issue
- **`the-byte-book-of-pascal`** — a book, not a magazine issue
- **Duplicates** — e.g. `byte-magazine-1988-07` AND `byte-magazine-1988-07_201802` are almost certainly the same issue scanned/uploaded twice
- **`byte-magazine-article-index-1975-11-1976-12`** and **`byte-magazine-atari-articles`** — these look like indexes/compilations, not single issues

**2. The good news:** there's one clean, dominant, well-structured block — everything matching `byte-magazine-YYYY-MM` (e.g., `byte-magazine-1975-09` through `byte-magazine-1995-09`). This matches the exact link format your A1 form already cited as your primary source. This should be your **primary pool** to pick from, since it's consistent and traceably matches what you already told the grader your corpus is.

## Next step: filter to that clean subset

Run this to isolate just the consistent `byte-magazine-YYYY-MM` items and count them:

```bash

```

```bash
ia search 'collection:byte-magazine' --itemlist | grep -E '^byte-magazine-[0-9]{4}-[0-9]{2}$' | wc -l
```

Run both and paste me the results. This will tell us:
- How many genuinely clean, consistently-named issues exist
- Whether that count is closer to your claimed 224 (if so, this confirms these are your "real" corpus and the other 129 items in the 353 are noise/duplicates/off-scope you should explicitly exclude and document)
- Any gaps in date coverage, so we pick a good spread for your 10–15 subset

This also matters for `data/provenance.md` — you'll want to explicitly state your inclusion/exclusion criteria (e.g., "we include only the `byte-magazine-YYYY-MM` naming pattern, excluding regional editions, listings supplements, and duplicate re-scans") so your corpus size claim is defensible under the Grounding Gate.

AI: This is genuinely important data — let's process it carefully before picking your subset.

## What this confirms

**176 clean, consistently-named issues** — not 224 as stated in your A1 form. This is a real discrepancy you need to address, not ignore.

**Coverage pattern (scanning the sorted list):**
- **1975–1984:** essentially complete, every month present — this is your strongest, most reliable block
- **1985:** missing January
- **1987:** missing October
- **1988:** missing January, February
- **1989:** only 01, 03, 04, 05, 08, 10, 12 present (missing 6 months)
- **1990:** only December present (missing 11 months!)
- **1991:** only Jan–Mar present, then nothing
- **1992:** missing June
- **1993:** only June and November present
- **1994:** completely absent from this clean pattern
- **1995:** only September present
- **1996–1998:** completely absent from this clean pattern (though messier variants like `byteme1996_*`, `byteme1997_*` exist in the full 353 — but recall those may be a different publication, not confirmed as BYTE proper)

## Why this matters for your project right now

1. **You cannot claim "the entire ~224-issue run" as literally indexed** if you're only using the clean-pattern subset — 176 ≠ 224. You have three options: (a) update your provenance language to say 176, (b) investigate whether some of the 129 "noisy" items in the full 353 are legitimately additional real issues worth including with cleanup, or (c) explicitly document that some months are permanently unavailable/unscanned on IA. Given your NFR is about scaling to the "full indexed corpus," this affects what "full" honestly means — worth flagging to Anisa and Sunanda now, since it touches the manifest/provenance language they're each responsible for.

2. **1990s coverage is thin** in the clean pattern — if your team wants representation across the whole 1975–1998 range (as your A1 domain framing implies — "how a specific machine... was covered at the time it launched"), you may need to accept some of the messier-named 1990s items later, which reintroduces the naming inconsistency problem for that portion.

## Next step: write `get_data.sh`

Now we build the actual script. Given your storage constraints, here's the plan:

1. **Hardcode your chosen 10–15 identifiers** as an array (not the full 176 — that's the "later" case)
2. **Use `ia download` with `--glob`** to fetch only the file types you need, skipping massive raw image sets
3. **Target `data/raw/`** as required by the pipeline contract

**Before I write this, I need one decision from you:** what file format do you actually want per issue?

Internet Archive typically stores each BYTE issue with multiple derivative files, commonly including:
- `*.pdf` — a single combined PDF (usually the most storage-efficient single-file option)
- `*_jp2.zip` — raw page-image scans (huge, likely 100s of MB to GB+ — avoid given your storage issue)
- `*_djvu.txt` — IA's own OCR text output (tiny, but this is IA's OCR, not yours — remember your pipeline is supposed to do its own OCR via Qwen2-VL per your A1 form)
- `*_abbyy.gz` — IA's structured OCR XML (also not your OCR)

**My recommendation:** download just the `*.pdf` per issue. Your `ingest/loader.py` can then extract page images from the PDF locally (your A1 Section 5 design table already says exactly this: *"Pages are extracted from the PDFs during preprocessing"*) — so this matches what you already committed to on paper, and keeps each download to one manageable file instead of a bloated raw-scan archive.

**Quick check before we script it — run this on one issue to see actual file sizes:**
```bash

```
or more simply, list all files and their sizes for one issue:
```bash
ia list byte-magazine-1983-12 --format=json | python3 -m json.tool
```

**455,438,031 bytes ≈ 434 MB** for just this one issue's PDF.

And this is likely to vary — some issues may be smaller/larger depending on page count and image density, so this isn't even a worst-case number.

## Now let's write `get_data.sh`

Here's the script, built around everything we've established:
- Your 14-issue subset (1975–1993 spread, including the 3 issues already referenced in your `tasks.jsonl`)
- Downloads only the PDF per issue (via `ia download --glob`)
- Writes into `data/raw/` (which now transparently lands on `/mnt/projects`)
- Structured so extending to the full 176 later is a small change, not a rewrite

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash

# A1 — fetch or recreate your scanned corpus into data/raw/

set -euo pipefail

RAW_DIR="data/raw"
mkdir -p "$RAW_DIR"

# Subset for A2 (14 issues, 1975-1993). Extend this array toward the
# full 176-issue byte-magazine-YYYY-MM set for later milestones.
ISSUES=(
  "byte-magazine-1975-09"
  "byte-magazine-1978-07"
  "byte-magazine-1980-07"
  "byte-magazine-1981-05"
  "byte-magazine-1982-04"
  "byte-magazine-1983-11"
  "byte-magazine-1983-12"
  "byte-magazine-1984-01"
  "byte-magazine-1985-06"
  "byte-magazine-1986-08"
  "byte-magazine-1987-09"
  "byte-magazine-1988-05"
  "byte-magazine-1989-04"
  "byte-magazine-1992-03"
  "byte-magazine-1993-06"
)

echo "Fetching ${#ISSUES[@]} BYTE issues into $RAW_DIR ..."

for id in "${ISSUES[@]}"; do
  echo "-> $id"
  ia download "$id" --glob="*.pdf" --destdir="$RAW_DIR" --no-directories
done

echo "Done. Contents of $RAW_DIR:"
ls -la "$RAW_DIR"
```

**A few things worth understanding before you run it:**

- `--no-directories` flattens IA's default per-item subfolder structure so all PDFs land directly in `data/raw/` rather than `data/raw/byte-magazine-1983-12/...` — simpler for `loader.py` to iterate over.
- `set -euo pipefail` (already in the stub) means the whole script stops immediately if any single download fails, rather than silently continuing with a partial corpus.
- Total expected size: 14 issues × roughly ~400MB average (based on your one sample) could land anywhere from 4–8GB depending on page density — comfortably within your 54GB free on `/mnt/projects`.

**Before running this on all 14, let's test it on just ONE issue first** to confirm the command syntax actually works end-to-end. Temporarily edit the `ISSUES` array to contain just `"byte-magazine-1983-12"`, save, then run:

```bash
bash scripts/get_data.sh
```

Paste me the output — once we confirm one issue downloads cleanly, we'll restore the full list and run it for real.

## Step 5: Push your branch to GitHub

Now it's safe to push — this is the first time this branch will exist on GitHub, so we need `-u` to set up the tracking link:

```bash
git push -u origin debashmita/get-data-provenance
```

**Why `-u` (`--set-upstream`)**: this tells git "this local branch corresponds to this remote branch" — after this one time, future `git push`/`git pull` on this branch will work without needing to specify `origin debashmita/get-data-provenance` every time.

Run it and paste the output. Once that's confirmed pushed, you'll have a genuinely complete, safe checkpoint: your `get_data.sh` work is committed, merged with the team's latest, and backed up on GitHub — separate from `main`, so nothing you're still actively testing (like the corpus download) can accidentally break anyone else's work.

AI: This is the exact authentication issue flagged back in the How-To-Submit handbook — GitHub no longer accepts your account password over HTTPS, so we need a **Personal Access Token (PAT)** instead. Let's set that up now.

## Step 1: Generate a Personal Access Token on GitHub

1. Go to **github.com** → click your profile picture (top right) → **Settings**
2. Scroll down the left sidebar to **Developer settings** (near the bottom)
3. Click **Personal access tokens** → **Tokens (classic)**
4. Click **Generate new token** → **Generate new token (classic)**
5. Give it a name (e.g., `doc-agent-G21-laptop`)
6. Set an expiration (30–90 days is reasonable for the course duration — you can regenerate later if it expires)
7. **Under "Select scopes," check the `repo` box** (this grants push access — the How-To-Submit handbook specifically said "with repo scope")
8. Scroll down, click **Generate token**
9. **Copy the token immediately** — GitHub shows it only once. It'll look like `ghp_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx`. Paste it somewhere temporarily safe (a text editor) — don't lose it, since you can't view it again.

## Step 2: Use the token as your password

Run the push command again:
```bash
git push -u origin debashmita/get-data-provenance
```

When prompted:
- **Username:** same as before (`2105098@ugrad.cse.buet.ac.bd`)
- **Password:** paste the **token** (not your actual GitHub password)

**One thing to know:** when you paste the token at the password prompt, your terminal won't show any characters or cursor movement — that's normal for password-style input, not a freeze. Just paste and press Enter.

## Optional but recommended: avoid re-entering this every time

If you don't want to paste the token on every single push, you can cache it:
```bash
git config --global credential.helper store
```
Run this once, before your next push — after entering the token successfully one time, git will remember it for future pushes on this machine.

AI: Confirmed exactly what I suspected — the file literally has **two full script bodies stacked on top of each other**:

1. **Lines 1–5:** the original stub (shebang, comments, `set -euo pipefail`, the `echo "TODO..."` placeholder)
2. **Lines 6 onward:** a second `#!/usr/bin/env bash` shebang, then our real logic

**Why this happened:** when I originally gave you the script content, it was likely appended to the end of the file rather than replacing the stub's contents — probably via a copy-paste or an editor action that added new lines below the existing ones instead of overwriting them.

**Why this needs fixing (not just cosmetic):**
1. A file can only have one valid shebang line (`#!/usr/bin/env bash`) — having two doesn't break bash execution (the second one is just treated as a comment when not on line 1), but it signals sloppy, unreviewed work to a grader.
2. The leftover `echo "TODO: fetch corpus into data/raw/"` actually **executes** every time you run the script — that's exactly why you saw `TODO: fetch corpus into data/raw/` print out before your real "Fetching 15 BYTE issues..." message when you first ran it. It's not just visual clutter; it's dead code silently running.
3. Per the Codebase Guide's rule — "you edit, you never create" — the expectation is a clean edit of the *existing* stub, not stub-plus-new-content bolted together.

## The fix

We'll rewrite the file cleanly, keeping only the real logic (lines 6 onward), removing the duplicate stub lines (1–5) entirely.That's the clean version — single shebang, no dead TODO line, comment count corrected to "15 issues" (matching your actual array, since your earlier comment said "14" but you actually have 15 entries).

## Step 2: Explore PDF-to-page-image extraction (using what you already have)

**Why this is the right next step, with reasoning:**

Your OCR benchmarking task (Day 2, Qwen2-VL vs. a lighter model) needs **page images**, not raw PDFs — a VLM-based OCR model reads pixels, not PDF structure. Your team's A1 form already committed to this exact plan (Table 13, Design facet): *"Pages are extracted from the PDFs during preprocessing"* rendered *"at 300 DPI via PyMuPDF."* So this step is: turn one PDF into individual page images, so we have something real to feed into OCR next.

We'll use `1975_09_BYTE_00-01_The_Worlds_Greatest_Toy.pdf` (the first, smallest one — 62MB) since a smaller file means faster iteration while we get the extraction logic right, before doing this at scale.

**First, check if PyMuPDF is installed** (your A1 form specifically committed to this library):

```bash
python3 -c "import fitz; print(fitz.__doc__)"
```

**Why check first, not just install blindly:** PyMuPDF's package name (`pymupdf` on PyPI) and its import name (`fitz`) are different, which trips people up — I want to confirm what's already there before adding anything, especially since we now know your `pip` situation needs the `python3 -m pip` + `--break-system-packages` pattern we established earlier.

Run that and paste the output — if it's not installed, we'll install it the same safe way we did for `internetarchive`.

Me: python3 -c "import fitz; print(fitz.__doc__)"
Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "<string>", line 1, in <module>
ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'fitz'

AI: Expected — not installed yet. Same pattern as before, so this should be quick.

## Install PyMuPDF

```bash
python3 -m pip install pymupdf --break-system-packages
```

**Why `pymupdf` as the install name but `fitz` as the import name:** this is just a historical naming quirk of the library — it was originally built as a Python binding to a C library called MuPDF, and `fitz` was the internal module name that stuck even as the PyPI package got renamed to `pymupdf` for clarity. Not something you're doing wrong — just how the package is structured upstream.

**Verify it installed correctly:**
```bash
python3 -c "import fitz; print(fitz.__doc__)"
```

This time it should print something like `PyMuPDF X.X.X: Python bindings for the MuPDF...` instead of erroring.

Run both and paste the output — once confirmed, we'll write a small test script to extract just the first page of your 1975-09 PDF as an image, so we can visually inspect one page before scaling this up to full issues.

## Step 3: Extract one test page as an image

Let's write a tiny standalone test script — not part of the real pipeline yet, just to prove the extraction works and let you *see* a page before we build this into `ingest/loader.py` properly (which is Anisa's file, but understanding this now helps you both).

**Why test on ONE page first, not the whole PDF:** if something's misconfigured (wrong DPI, wrong output format), you want to discover that by inspecting one image, not by generating 100+ pages of a whole issue and finding out afterward. Fail fast, on the smallest possible unit.

Create a small test script:

```bash
mkdir -p /tmp/page_test
python3 << 'EOF'
import pymupdf

pdf_path = "data/raw/1975_09_BYTE_00-01_The_Worlds_Greatest_Toy.pdf"
doc = pymupdf.open(pdf_path)

print(f"Total pages in PDF: {len(doc)}")

# Render just the first page at 300 DPI, per your A1 form's stated plan
page = doc[0]
pix = page.get_pixmap(dpi=300)
pix.save("/tmp/page_test/page_001.png")

print(f"Saved page 1 as PNG — dimensions: {pix.width}x{pix.height}")
doc.close()
EOF
```

**What each part does:**
- `pymupdf.open(pdf_path)` — opens the PDF, gives you access to all its pages
- `doc[0]` — grabs the *first* page (0-indexed, so page 1 in human terms)
- `get_pixmap(dpi=300)` — renders that page as an actual image at 300 DPI, matching your A1 form's stated resolution choice
- `pix.save(...)` — writes it out as a viewable PNG file

Run this and paste me the output — it should print the total page count for this issue (useful data point — first issue's actual length) and confirm the image dimensions. Once we see it worked, we'll open the image to visually check it actually looks like a real BYTE page.

Me: Ok, before that, I need  total page count and word count of the full corpus, my teammate is asking for this

AI: Good question — but let's be precise about what's actually feasible here, because full corpus page/word counts for all 176 issues have two very different costs.

## The key distinction

**Page count** — cheap and easy. Internet Archive stores page count as metadata (usually a field like `imagecount`) that you can query **without downloading anything**. Just an API call per issue.

**Word count** — expensive. There's no metadata field for this; to know the actual word count, you'd need the *actual text content* of every issue, which means either:
- Downloading and parsing all 176 PDFs (potentially 30-60GB+ based on what we've seen — not viable given your storage situation), or
- Using IA's own OCR text layer (`_djvu.txt` files, mentioned earlier) — smaller than full PDFs, but still 176 separate downloads, and it's *their* OCR quality, not yours

## My recommendation: get real page counts now, estimate word count

This mirrors exactly what your own A1 form already did honestly — Table 7 said *"Rough estimate: ~45,000–50,000 pages total, ~20–25 million words"* — an estimate, not a measured count. For A2, we can upgrade the **page count** to a real, precise number (since it's free to get), while keeping word count as a **documented estimate** based on your actual downloaded sample's average words/page — which is more defensible than a guess, and honest about being an estimate.

AI: Ah, simpler than I made it — good, let's scope this down to just your working subset of 15. This is fast and cheap since we're only querying metadata for issues you already have.

## Get page counts for your 15 issues

```bash
for id in byte-magazine-1975-09 byte-magazine-1978-07 byte-magazine-1980-07 byte-magazine-1981-05 byte-magazine-1982-04 byte-magazine-1983-11 byte-magazine-1983-12 byte-magazine-1984-01 byte-magazine-1985-06 byte-magazine-1986-08 byte-magazine-1987-09 byte-magazine-1988-05 byte-magazine-1989-04 byte-magazine-1992-03 byte-magazine-1993-06; do
  count=$(ia metadata "$id" | python3 -c "import json,sys; d=json.load(sys.stdin); print(d.get('metadata',{}).get('imagecount','N/A'))")
  echo "$id : $count pages"
done
```

**What this does:**
- Loops through your exact 15 issue identifiers
- For each, queries IA's metadata (a tiny JSON response, not the PDF itself — no storage cost)
- Pulls out the `imagecount` field, which IA uses to record how many page-images exist for that scanned item
- Prints a clean `identifier : page count` line for each

**Why `imagecount` specifically:** this is the field IA uses across its scanned-book/magazine items to record the actual number of page-image scans — it should closely match (or exactly match) what you'd get from `len(doc)` in PyMuPDF (we already confirmed 100 pages for 1975-09 this way — worth checking if this metadata number agrees).

Run it — it'll take maybe 15-30 seconds total (one small API call per issue) — and paste me the full output. Once we have all 15, we can sum them for your subset total, and separately calculate a word-count estimate using the one real PDF we've already extracted from.

---

## 2. Execution Order: OCR Build → Run → Benchmark

Here's the correct step-by-step sequence, and the reasoning for why it must happen in this order — not skipped or reordered:

**Step 1 — Get layout regions first (dependency, not your file, but you need the output)**
Per the Codebase Guide, `vision/ocr.py`'s `transcribe_region(region)` takes a **`Region`**, not a raw page. Regions come from `vision/layout.py → detect()` — Sunanda's file. So technically, real OCR in the pipeline runs *after* layout detection, on segmented regions, not whole pages.
*Practical implication for you:* until Sunanda's `layout.py` is ready, you can still start OCR benchmarking using **whole rendered page images as a simplification** (what we did in the `/tmp` PyMuPDF test) — just be aware this is a shortcut for early benchmarking, and the real pipeline integration will need to consume `Region` objects once her code is ready.

**Step 2 — Build/configure the `Reader` in `vision/ocr.py`**
This means implementing the `Reader` class and its `transcribe_region()` method — wiring up your chosen model (e.g., Qwen2-VL-2B, per your A1 form) so it can take an image and return text. At this stage you're writing the actual OCR logic, not yet judging its quality.

**Step 3 — Run it on a handful of sample pages (not the whole corpus yet)**
Before committing to one model, run your candidate OCR model(s) on just a few pages — ideally ones you've already hand-transcribed into `grading_kit/heldout_pages/labels.jsonl` (ground truth). This is your benchmarking comparison step: run Qwen2-VL-2B and your lighter/faster candidate on the *same* sample pages, so you can compare outputs side by side.

**Step 4 — Measure quality (CER/WER) using `eval/metrics.py → ocr_f1()`**
Only once you have both (a) your OCR model's output text and (b) the real hand-transcribed ground truth from `labels.jsonl` can you actually compute a quality number. This is where `ocr_f1()` (or a CER/WER equivalent) gets called — comparing predicted text against gold text for the same pages.

**Step 5 — Finalize your model choice based on that comparison**
Whichever candidate scores better (and fits your speed/resource constraints — relevant for your "scalable" NFR) becomes your final choice. Per your Day 3 plan, you then run `transcribe()` across the **whole subset**, not just the sample.

**Step 6 — Only THEN build `notebooks/kb_demo.ipynb`**
The notebook's job (per the A2 form and Codebase Guide) is to **display evidence** — show OCR quality and one retrieval example — not to do the benchmarking work itself. The notebook comes last: it's a presentation layer over results you've already produced and validated in Steps 3–5.

**Why this order matters, not just as convention:** if you build the notebook before finalizing your OCR model, you'd have to redo the notebook every time you swap models during benchmarking. Benchmark first, decide, then document — one clean pass, not repeated rework.

Me: cat src/doc_agent/contracts.py
"""FIXED data contracts. Do not change fields or types."""
from __future__ import annotations
from pydantic import BaseModel
class Page(BaseModel):
    id: str
    image_path: str
    doc_id: str
class Region(BaseModel):
    page_id: str
    bbox: tuple[int, int, int, int]
    kind: str  # text | table | figure | heading
class Chunk(BaseModel):
    id: str
    doc_id: str
    text: str
    page_ids: list[str]
    score: float = 0.0        # relevance score set by retrieval; decide() reads the top score to judge weak evidence
class Query(BaseModel):
    text: str
    verifiable: bool          # True = checkable by exact match (anchors objective grading + RLVR)
    judged: bool              # True = scored by LLM-judge / human (non-verifiable inference)
class Citation(BaseModel):
    chunk_id: str
    span: tuple[int, int]
class Answer(BaseModel):
    text: str
    citations: list[Citation]
    grounded: bool
    confidence: float
class ToolResult(BaseModel):
    ok: bool
    payload: dict
class TraceStep(BaseModel):
    """One agent step, emitted to traces/run.jsonl so the A3 agentic-feature check can read the path."""
    step: int
    tool: str                 # tool name, or "decide" / "answer"
    args: dict                # e.g. {"query": "..."} — for retrieve, the query actually used
    obs: dict                 # what decide() saw, e.g. {"top_score": 0.31, "n": 10}
# MANDATORY agentic behaviour (graded in A3): evidence-gated re-search — decide() must re-retrieve with a
# reformulated query when the top evidence is weak. That runtime branch is what makes the system an agent.
# (multi-hop -> recall NFR, verify-and-correct -> precision NFR, tool-routing -> bonus; see the codebase guide.)

## What `contracts.py` tells us for your OCR work

A few important, concrete details for when you write `Reader.transcribe_region()`:

1. **`Region.bbox` is `tuple[int, int, int, int]`** — standard bounding-box format (likely x0, y0, x1, y1). Your OCR function will need to crop the page image to this box before running the model on it.

This matters for your OCR design decision: do you run the same OCR model on all region kinds, or only on `text`/`heading` regions and handle `table`/`figure` differently? 4. **No `Region.text` field yet** — meaning OCR output isn't stored back into the `Region` object itself; it must flow into a `Chunk` (which has a `text: str` field) via `index/chunk.py`. So your OCR pipeline conceptually is: `Region` (with bbox) → your `transcribe_region()` → raw text string → eventually becomes part of a `Chunk`.

5. **`Chunk.score`** confirms what the agentic re-search logic reads later (Stage 6) — not directly your concern now, but useful context for why this field exists.

## Before we write the OCR benchmarking script — one important checkpoint

We're about to work with **Qwen2-VL-2B**, per your A1 form's OCR model choice. Before writing any code, we need to address something directly connected to the storage problem we already hit tonight: **this is a real, multi-gigabyte model.** A 2B-parameter vision-language model's weights typically run several GB on disk (often 4-6GB+ depending on precision), separate entirely from your corpus data.

Given we already found your root partition (`/`) sitting at 98% full with only 1.5GB free, **downloading Qwen2-VL-2B's weights to the default location could immediately fail or fill your disk again** — same problem as before, different cause.

**Two things to check before proceeding:**

**1. Where would the model weights actually go by default?**
If you're planning to use it via Hugging Face's `transformers` library, weights typically cache to `~/.cache/huggingface/` — which lives on your home directory, i.e., your nearly-full root partition. We'd want to redirect this to `/mnt/projects` the same way we did for `data/raw/`, *before* downloading anything.

**2. Do you actually want to run Qwen2-VL locally at all, given your hardware?**
A 2B-parameter VLM also needs real compute to run inference at reasonable speed — do you know if your laptop has a dedicated GPU, or would this be running on CPU? This matters a lot for whether local benchmarking is even practical tonight, versus using an API-based route instead.

Is the model needed for getting the ocr?
Maybe running in my machine is not effective and it is risky maybe given the GPU situation.  Can I run it somewhere else like Kaggle or Google Colab?

AI: Confirmed — no NVIDIA GPU on this machine (that error means the driver/tooling isn't installed at all, and given your storage constraints too, this machine really isn't set up for local VLM inference). Good that you asked before we tried to download a multi-GB model onto an already-strained 1.5GB-free root partition — that would've been a real problem.

## 1. Is the model needed for getting the OCR?

**Yes — the model IS the OCR mechanism**, not an optional add-on. Per your A1 form, Qwen2-VL-2B is your chosen approach for `Reader.transcribe_region()` — a vision-language model that takes a region/page image and outputs the text it reads. Without running *some* model (Qwen2-VL or your "lighter/faster candidate" alternative), there's no OCR step happening at all — `transcribe()` would have nothing to call.

**But — important distinction:** you don't need to run it *locally on your laptop* for it to count as your OCR component. What matters for the pipeline is that `Reader.transcribe_region()` produces text output; *where* the model's computation actually happens (your laptop vs. a cloud notebook vs. an API) is an implementation detail, not a contract requirement.

## 2. Can you run it on Kaggle or Google Colab instead?

**Yes, and given your hardware situation, this is clearly the better path.** Both platforms give you free GPU access, which solves your two biggest local blockers at once — no storage crunch (model weights live on their servers/disk, not yours) and no compute bottleneck (they provide actual GPUs, unlike your CPU-only laptop).

**How this fits your actual pipeline, practically:**

- **Google Colab** (free tier includes a T4 GPU) — you'd write a notebook there, install `transformers`, download Qwen2-VL-2B onto Colab's own disk (not yours), upload a few sample page images (small, just for benchmarking), run inference, and copy the output text back.
- **Kaggle Notebooks** — similar setup, also free GPU quota, sometimes more generous session limits than Colab's free tier.

**Practical workflow for your benchmarking step specifically:**
1. Extract a handful of sample page images locally (we already proved this works — the `/tmp` PyMuPDF test)
2. Upload just those few sample images to Colab/Kaggle (small, no storage concern)
3. Run Qwen2-VL-2B (and your lighter candidate) on them there
4. Compare outputs against your `labels.jsonl` ground truth — compute CER/WER
5. Bring the results (not the model) back to your local repo — i.e., your `eval/metrics.py → ocr_f1()` call and the final chosen approach get documented/wired locally, but the actual heavy model doesn't need to live on your laptop at all

**One thing worth deciding with your team:** if Qwen2-VL genuinely needs to run at *inference time* in your final deployed system (not just for one-time benchmarking), you'll eventually need a plan for where that live inference happens in the real pipeline too (A4's serving stage) — but that's a later-milestone concern, not blocking your A2 benchmarking work right now.

**Which platform do you want to set up — Colab or Kaggle?** (Colab is generally simpler to start with if you haven't used either before.)

## Why this specific file might be a persistent problem

A few possibilities:
1. **The file itself is unusually large** — we don't actually know its size yet (unlike 1983-12, which we confirmed was 434MB). If this one is significantly bigger, it's more likely to exceed whatever timeout threshold is causing the drops.
2. **This particular IA server node consistently struggles** — though it hit two different servers (`ia801600` and `ia601609`) across attempts, so probably not one broken node specifically.
3. **Your network has a consistent issue with long-duration single connections** — shorter files (like your first 5, all under ~200MB) succeeded fine; the pattern of failures is concentrated on your two largest files (`1982-04` at 512MB succeeded, but `1983-11` and `1983-12` have both failed at least once).

## Let's check the actual file size first

```bash
ia metadata byte-magazine-1983-11 | python3 -c "
import json,sys
d = json.load(sys.stdin)
for f in d.get('files', []):
    if f['name'].endswith('.pdf'):
        print(f['name'], f.get('size', 'unknown'))
"
```

This tells us definitively whether this file is just unusually large, which would explain the repeated timeouts on a shaky connection.

## Practical fix: add a retry loop with resume, instead of manual single attempts

Rather than you re-running the command by hand each time, let's make the download self-healing:

```bash
for i in 1 2 3 4 5; do
  ia download byte-magazine-1983-11 --glob="*.pdf" --destdir="data/raw" --no-directories && break
  echo "Attempt $i failed, retrying in 10s..."
  sleep 10
done
```

**Why this works:** `ia download` skips/resumes files that already partially exist (we saw this "skipping... already exists" behavior earlier) — so each retry picks up further along, not from zero, since the previous attempt did complete some portion before timing out. The `&& break` stops the loop the moment one attempt succeeds; if all 5 fail, at least you'll see it tried multiple times with pauses in between rather than one-off attempts.

Run the metadata check first (quick, informative), then run the retry loop and let it work through its attempts — paste me both outputs.

        "def extract_page(pdf_path, page_num, output_path, dpi=300):\n",
        "    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)\n",
        "    page = doc[page_num - 1]  # PyMuPDF is 0-indexed\n",
        "    zoom = dpi / 72\n",
        "    mat = fitz.Matrix(zoom, zoom)\n",

        "    pix.save(output_path)\n",

        "\n",
        "    entry = {\"page_id\": page_id, \"text\": text}\n",
        "    with open(LABELS_PATH, \"a\", encoding=\"utf-8\") as f:\n",
        "        f.write(json.dumps(entry, ensure_ascii=False) + \"\\n\")\n",
        "    print(f\"✅ Saved label for {page_id} ({len(text.split())} words)\")\n",
        "\n",
        "\n",
        "def show_page(page_id, images_dir=\"grading_kit/heldout_pages\"):\n",
        "    \"\"\"Display a heldout page so you can transcribe it while looking at it.\"\"\"\n",
        "    img_path = f\"{images_dir}/{page_id}.png\"\n",
        "    img = Image.open(img_path)\n",
        "    display(img)\n",
        "    print(f\"Size: {img.size}\")"
      ],
      "metadata": {
        "id": "mXFTCgANIePW"

      "execution_count": null,

    {
      "cell_type": "code",
      "source": [],

        "id": "uZ4Z_N3jI0lZ"

      "execution_count": null,
      "outputs": []

    {
      "cell_type": "code",
      "source": [
        "import fitz\n",
        "from PIL import Image\n",
        "import io\n",
        "\n",
        "def extract_page_image_direct(pdf_path, page_num, output_path):\n",
        "    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)\n",
        "    page = doc[page_num - 1]\n",
        "    images = page.get_images(full=True)\n",
        "\n",
        "    if not images:\n",
        "        print(f\"⚠️ No embedded image found on page {page_num}\")\n",
        "        doc.close()\n",
        "        return False\n",
        "\n",
        "    xref = max(images, key=lambda im: im[2] * im[3])[0]\n",
        "    pix = fitz.Pixmap(doc, xref)\n",
        "\n",
        "    if pix.n - pix.alpha >= 4:\n",
        "        pix = fitz.Pixmap(fitz.csRGB, pix)\n",
        "\n",
        "    # Force through PIL to guarantee genuine PNG bytes, regardless of source format\n",
        "    img_bytes = pix.tobytes(\"ppm\")   # PPM is a safe lossless intermediate fitz supports directly\n",
        "    img = Image.open(io.BytesIO(img_bytes))\n",
        "    img.save(output_path, format=\"PNG\")   # explicit format, not inferred from extension\n",
        "\n",
        "    doc.close()\n",
        "    return True"
      ],
      "metadata": {
        "id": "Wg3qHc6GAAcR"
      },

## Two issues worth catching first

**1. A naming mismatch in your first extraction cell:**
```python
extract_page(

    13,
```

**2. Two different extraction methods in the notebook** — `extract_page()` (renders the whole page via `get_pixmap`) vs. `extract_page_image_direct()` (pulls the largest *embedded image* directly from the PDF's internal image objects). These can produce different results — the second one only grabs an embedded raster image and skips any text that exists as vector/font data on the page, which could **cut off real article text** if the PDF mixes embedded scan images with separate text layers. For consistency and to avoid silently losing content, I'd recommend sticking with the **first method** (`extract_page`, using `get_pixmap`) for your real pipeline — it renders the whole page faithfully, which is also literally what your A1 form committed to ("Pages are extracted from the PDFs during preprocessing... via PyMuPDF").


**Reasoning for page selection:** ads in old magazines are typically concentrated in the front matter (first 10-20%) and back matter (last 10-15%) — feature articles cluster in the middle. import fitz

```bash
pages_to_try = [
    ("data/raw/1989_04_BYTE_14-04_CASE_and_UPSes_and_Graphics_Suppliment.pdf", 180, "grading_kit/heldout_pages/byte-magazine-1989-04_p180.png"),
    ("data/raw/1992_03_BYTE_17-03_Memory_and_Storage_Advances.pdf", 170, "grading_kit/heldout_pages/byte-magazine-1992-03_p170.png"),
    ("data/raw/byte-magazine-1993-06.pdf", 140, "grading_kit/heldout_pages/byte-magazine-1993-06_p140.png"),
]

for pdf_path, page_num, output_path in pages_to_try:
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    page = doc[page_num - 1]
    pix = page.get_pixmap(dpi=300)
    pix.save(output_path)
    print(f"Saved: {output_path} ({pix.width}x{pix.height})")
    doc.close()
EOF
```

AI: Confirmed exactly what I suspected: real, raw newlines (`$` marking each line end in `cat -A` output) sitting **inside** the JSON string value, un-escaped. This is invalid JSON — a proper JSONL entry must be a single line, with any actual line breaks inside the text represented as the two-character escape sequence `\n`, not a literal newline.

**Scope of the damage:** `wc -l` shows 273 lines total for what should be a small number of JSON *entries* (one per page) — this strongly suggests **most or all entries have this same problem**, since one legitimate multi-paragraph transcription is spanning many raw lines instead of being one JSON line.

## This needs a fix before anyone can use this file for anything

`eval/metrics.py → ocr_f1()` (whoever writes it — sounds like your team's plan has this under your name still) would fail immediately trying to parse this file line-by-line. This blocks real progress, not just a cosmetic issue.

**Let's write a small repair script.** The safest approach: read the whole file, detect that entries are broken across multiple raw lines, and reconstruct/re-escape them properly.

```bash
python3 << 'EOF'
import re

with open("grading_kit/labels.jsonl", "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    content = f.read()

# Split into entries based on the pattern: each entry starts with {"page_id":
entries = re.split(r'(?=\{"page_id")', content)
entries = [e.strip() for e in entries if e.strip()]

print(f"Found {len(entries)} raw entry blocks")
for e in entries[:3]:
    print("---")
    print(repr(e[:150]))
EOF
```

**Why this diagnostic step first, before writing a fix:** I want to see the exact raw structure of a few entries (using `repr()` to show hidden characters clearly) before committing to a repair strategy — malformed files can have inconsistent damage, and I'd rather confirm the pattern holds across multiple entries than assume and risk making it worse.

Run this and paste the output — once we see the real structure, we'll write a proper repair script that reconstructs valid JSONL, then you can flag the root cause to whoever wrote it so future entries are added correctly (likely they need to type `\n` literally or use a proper JSON-writing method instead of hand-editing raw text into the file).

## Cell 1: Corpus inventory (add this as a new code cell, after the existing `# IMPLEMENT` cell)

```python
import fitz
import os
import glob

RAW_DIR = "data/raw"
pdf_files = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(RAW_DIR, "*.pdf")))

print(f"Issues currently downloaded: {len(pdf_files)}")
print()

total_pages = 0
issue_summary = []

for pdf_path in pdf_files:
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    n_pages = len(doc)
    total_pages += n_pages
    issue_summary.append((os.path.basename(pdf_path), n_pages))
    doc.close()

for name, pages in issue_summary:
    print(f"  {name}: {pages} pages")

print()
print(f"TOTAL pages across {len(pdf_files)} downloaded issues: {total_pages}")
print(f"Meets 300-page minimum? {'YES' if total_pages >= 300 else 'NO'}")
```

**Why each part is here, briefly:**
- `glob.glob(...*.pdf)` — scans whatever's actually in `data/raw/` right now, satisfying your "first approach" choice; this cell will just show more issues as your download finishes, no code changes needed later
- Per-issue breakdown, not just a total — useful for `data/provenance.md` and for spotting outliers (e.g., if the interrupted `1988-05` file is corrupted, you'd see it here as a low/wrong page count)
- The explicit 300-page check — ties directly back to your A1 form's committed minimum, gives a clear pass/fail signal a grader can see at a glance

**Go ahead and add this cell, run it, and paste me the output** — once we see real numbers, we'll move to Cell 2 (word count estimate) and Cell 3 (visual multi-column evidence), one at a time as you asked.

**Why this one, not the others:**
- **"Python Environments..."** — this lets you pick a specific local Python installation on your machine. Since we've been installing packages (`pymupdf`, `internetarchive`) directly into your system's Python 3.12 via `python3 -m pip install ... --break-system-packages`, you want the notebook running on that **same** Python installation — otherwise it won't have access to `fitz`/`pymupdf` and you'll get a `ModuleNotFoundError` when you try to run the cell.
- **"Jupyter Kernel..."** — this is for selecting a kernel from an already-installed Jupyter kernel spec list; more relevant if you have multiple separate Jupyter kernels configured, which you likely don't yet.
- **"Existing Jupyter Server..."** — this is for connecting to a Jupyter server already running elsewhere (e.g., a remote server or one you started manually in a terminal) — not your situation right now.

**After clicking "Python Environments...", it should show you a list** — pick whichever one shows as your system Python 3.12 (likely labeled something like `/usr/bin/python3` or similar, possibly marked as the "global" or default environment). **Now, Cell 2 — word count estimate.** Since exact word counts for all 176 issues isn't feasible, this extends Cell 1's loop to extract text per PDF using `fitz`/`pymupdf` and count words for your actual downloaded subset:

```python
total_words = 0
word_summary = []

for pdf_path in pdf_files:
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    issue_words = 0
    for page in doc:
        text = page.get_text()
        issue_words += len(text.split())
    doc.close()
    word_summary.append((os.path.basename(pdf_path), issue_words))
    total_words += issue_words

for name, words in word_summary:
    print(f"  {name}: {words:,} words")

print()
print(f"TOTAL words across {len(pdf_files)} downloaded issues: {total_words:,}")
print(f"Meets 60,000-word minimum? {'YES' if total_words >= 60000 else 'NO'}")
```

**Why this way, briefly:**
- `page.get_text()` pulls the PDF's embedded text layer directly — no OCR needed yet, since these are Internet Archive scans that typically already carry an OCR'd text layer (IA runs its own OCR at upload time). This is just for your exploration/word-count estimate, not your project's actual OCR pipeline (that's still your `vision/ocr.py` / Qwen2-VL work, which reads the *images*, not this embedded layer).
- Per-issue breakdown again, for the same reason as Cell 1 — spotting outliers (an issue with suspiciously few words might mean IA's text layer failed on it, which is itself useful to know).

This will likely take a bit longer to run than Cell 1 since it's extracting full text per page across ~4,000 pages — that's normal, just let it finish. Paste the output when it's done and we'll move to Cell 3 (visual multi-column evidence).

```python
import fitz
import os

# Pick a page known to be dense text/multi-column - adjust page_num if needed
sample_pdf = os.path.join(RAW_DIR, "1983_11_BYTE_08-11_Inside_the_IBM_PC.pdf")
doc = fitz.open(sample_pdf)

# Render a handful of pages as images so we can visually confirm multi-column layout
os.makedirs("../grading_kit/heldout_pages/preview", exist_ok=True)

pages_to_check = [20, 50, 100]  # early spot-check; adjust once you see output

for page_num in pages_to_check:
    if page_num < len(doc):
        page = doc[page_num]
        pix = page.get_pixmap(dpi=150)
        out_path = f"../grading_kit/heldout_pages/preview/1983-11_p{page_num}.png"
        pix.save(out_path)
        print(f"Saved page {page_num} -> {out_path}")

doc.close()
```

```markdown
## Visual evidence: multi-column layout

Pages 20, 50, and 100 of `1983-11 (Inside the IBM PC)` all show a multi-column
body-text layout typical of BYTE's editorial pages. This confirms the data
speciality is genuinely present in the corpus, not just a category label.

**Consequence for the pipeline:** naive top-to-bottom, left-to-right text
extraction (e.g. plain `page.get_text()`) will interleave column content
incorrectly, splicing lines from column 1 with lines from column 2 mid-sentence.
This forces a layout-detection stage (`vision/layout.py`) that identifies
column boundaries *before* OCR/text extraction runs, so each column is read
top-to-bottom independently and reassembled in the correct order.
```

AI: Let's add the page-size/dimension spread check next.

**Cell 4 — page dimensions across the corpus:**

```python
dims = []

for pdf_path in pdf_files:
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    for page in doc:
        rect = page.rect
        dims.append((os.path.basename(pdf_path), rect.width, rect.height))
    doc.close()

widths = [d[1] for d in dims]
heights = [d[2] for d in dims]

print(f"Total pages measured: {len(dims)}")
print(f"Width  range: {min(widths):.1f} - {max(widths):.1f} pt")
print(f"Height range: {min(heights):.1f} - {max(heights):.1f} pt")
print(f"Median width:  {sorted(widths)[len(widths)//2]:.1f} pt")
print(f"Median height: {sorted(heights)[len(heights)//2]:.1f} pt")

# Flag pages whose dimensions are unusual outliers vs the median - a rough
# signal for cropped/misscanned pages or inserted supplements of a different size
from statistics import median
med_w, med_h = median(widths), median(heights)
outliers = [d for d in dims if abs(d[1]-med_w) > 50 or abs(d[2]-med_h) > 50]
print(f"\nPages with unusual dimensions (>50pt off median): {len(outliers)}")
for name, w, h in outliers[:10]:
    print(f"  {name}: {w:.1f} x {h:.1f}")
```

**Why this matters for your build, specifically:**

- BYTE issues sometimes bind in **supplements or foldout ads at a different trim size** than the regular editorial pages — this check catches that before it becomes a silent bug in `ingest/loader.py` (e.g. a layout model trained/tuned assuming one consistent page size misreading an odd-sized insert).
- Since your NFR is **scalability (p95 latency ≤ 1.5s)**, a wildly inconsistent page size distribution could also matter for batching decisions later in `vision/layout.py` or `vision/ocr.py` — fixed-size batching assumptions break if pages vary a lot.
- This is a genuinely different finding from Cell 3's multi-column evidence — Section 3 of A2 wants **three distinct** findings, not three versions of the same one, so this rounds that out nicely alongside "multi-column layout is real" and your word-count spread.

Run it and paste the output — pt values will look like roughly 570–620 wide / 790–830 tall if it's fairly consistent (standard US letter-ish scan size), or you'll see it clearly if something's an outlier.

what do you mean by outlier here

AI: An "outlier" here just means: a page whose width or height sits more than 50 points away from the corpus-wide median (556.8pt × 779.0pt) — that 50pt threshold was an arbitrary cutoff I picked to flag pages that are *meaningfully* different in size, not just normal scan-to-scan jitter of a few points.

**Now that you have the full breakdown, the picture is more interesting than my first guess** — it's actually two different things mixed together:

**1. `1978-07` — 212 outlier pages out of 212 total pages.** Every single page in that issue is flagged. This confirms the "whole issue scanned at a different trim/DPI setting" case — a provenance/consistency finding, not a within-issue problem.

**2. Every other flagged issue — only a fraction of its pages are outliers**, e.g. `1982-04`: 126 out of 548, `1985-06`: 30 out of 540, `1983-11`: 17 out of 720. This is different — it means *most* pages in these issues match the median size just fine, but a handful of individual pages within the same issue don't. That's much more consistent with **foldout ads, inserts, or oversized diagram pages** bound into an otherwise normal-size issue — exactly the "supplement at a different trim size" case this check was actually designed to catch.

**So the honest finding is really two findings, not one:**
- Whole-issue scan-setting inconsistency exists (`1978-07`).
- Individual oversized/undersized inserts exist scattered across most other issues too.

**Markdown cell to add**, capturing this accurately:

```markdown
## Finding: page dimensions are not fully uniform across the corpus

Of 6,603 pages, 451 (~6.8%) differ from the corpus median (556.8 x 779.0 pt)
by more than 50pt in width or height. Breaking this down by issue reveals
two distinct causes:

1. **Whole-issue scan differences** - `1978-07` has ALL 212 of its pages
   flagged, meaning that entire issue was scanned at a different trim/DPI
   setting than the rest of the corpus.
2. **Within-issue inserts** - every other flagged issue has only a fraction
   of its pages flagged (e.g. `1982-04`: 126/548, `1985-06`: 30/540),
   consistent with foldout ads or oversized diagram pages bound into an
   otherwise normal-size issue.

**Consequence for the pipeline:** `ingest/loader.py` cannot assume a single
fixed page size for the whole corpus, or even for a whole issue. Any
downstream step that resizes/normalizes pages (before layout detection or
OCR) needs to read each page's actual dimensions individually rather than
hardcoding one expected size - relevant to scalability (our NFR), since a
naive fixed-size batching assumption would silently mishandle ~7% of pages.
```

This is a genuinely good third finding for Section 3 — distinct from the multi-column layout finding, concrete, honest about the two different causes rather than papering over the discrepancy, and it ties directly to your NFR the way the walkthrough wants.

```python
# Cell 5 - check for pages with little/no embedded text (candidates for OCR failure/blank scans)
low_text_pages = []

for pdf_path in pdf_files:
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    for i, page in enumerate(doc):
        word_count = len(page.get_text().split())
        if word_count < 10:  # near-empty text layer
            low_text_pages.append((os.path.basename(pdf_path), i, word_count))
    doc.close()

print(f"Pages with <10 words in embedded text layer: {len(low_text_pages)} / {len(dims)}")
for name, page_num, wc in low_text_pages[:15]:
    print(f"  {name} page {page_num}: {wc} words")
```
This will likely catch cover pages, full-page ads/images, and blank pages — useful to know, and directly justifies why your `vision/ocr.py` stage exists rather than just reusing IA's text layer.
