"""Stage 3 — OCR using Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct."""
from __future__ import annotations
from pathlib import Path
import torch
import json 
from PIL import Image
from ..contracts import Chunk, Region

OCR_DIR = Path("data/interim/ocr")

_model = None
_processor = None

def _get_model(cfg: dict):
    """Load the configured VLM once and reuse it."""

    global _model, _processor

    if _model is not None and _processor is not None:
        return _model, _processor

    from transformers import (
        AutoProcessor,
        Qwen2VLForConditionalGeneration,
    )

    model_name = cfg["ocr"]["model"]

    print(f"Loading OCR model: {model_name}")

    _processor = AutoProcessor.from_pretrained(model_name)

    _model = Qwen2VLForConditionalGeneration.from_pretrained(
        model_name,
        torch_dtype="auto",
        device_map="auto",
    )

    _model.eval()

    return _model, _processor


def _find_page_image(page_id: str) -> Path:
    """Find the preprocessed page image belonging to a Region."""

    if "_p" not in page_id:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Unexpected page_id format: {page_id}"
        )

    doc_id, page_number = page_id.rsplit("_p", 1)

    filename = f"page_{page_number}.png"

    search_dirs = [
        Path("data/interim/preprocessed"),
        Path("data/interim/pages")
    ]

    for directory in search_dirs:
        candidate = directory / doc_id / filename

        if candidate.exists():
            return candidate

    # Fallback for slightly different directory structures.
    for directory in search_dirs:
        if directory.exists():
            matches = list(directory.rglob(filename))

            for path in matches:
                if doc_id in str(path):
                    return path

    raise FileNotFoundError(
        f"Could not find image for page_id={page_id}"
    )


def _crop_region(region: Region) -> Image.Image:
    """Load a page and crop the detected region."""

    image_path = _find_page_image(region.page_id)

    with Image.open(image_path) as image:
        image = image.convert("RGB")

        x0, y0, x1, y1 = region.bbox

        # Keep bbox inside image boundaries.
        x0 = max(0, min(x0, image.width))
        y0 = max(0, min(y0, image.height))
        x1 = max(0, min(x1, image.width))
        y1 = max(0, min(y1, image.height))

        if x1 <= x0 or y1 <= y0:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Invalid region bbox: {region.bbox}"
            )

        return image.crop((x0, y0, x1, y1))


def _prompt_for_region(kind: str) -> str:
    """Return an OCR instruction appropriate for the region type."""

    if kind == "heading":
        return (
            "Transcribe the heading exactly as shown. "
            "Return only the text. Do not explain or summarize."
        )

    if kind == "table":
        return (
            "Transcribe all readable text in this table. "
            "Preserve rows and columns as clearly as possible. "
            "Return only the transcription."
        )

    if kind == "figure":
        return (
            "Transcribe any readable text appearing in this figure, "
            "diagram, or caption. Return only the text. "
            "Do not describe the image."
        )

    return (
        "Transcribe all visible text exactly as shown. "
        "Preserve words, numbers, punctuation, and line breaks "
        "where possible. Do not summarize or explain."
    )


class Reader:
    """VLM-based OCR reader."""

    def __init__(self, cfg: dict) -> None:
        self.cfg = cfg["ocr"]
        self.model, self.processor = _get_model(cfg)

    def transcribe_region(self, region: Region) -> str:
        """OCR a single detected layout region."""

        crop = _crop_region(region)
        prompt = _prompt_for_region(region.kind)

        messages = [
            {
                "role": "user",
                "content": [
                    {
                        "type": "image",
                        "image": crop,
                    },
                    {
                        "type": "text",
                        "text": prompt,
                    },
                ],
            }
        ]

        inputs = self.processor.apply_chat_template(
            messages,
            add_generation_prompt=True,
            tokenize=True,
            return_dict=True,
            return_tensors="pt",
        )

        # Move tensor inputs to the same device as the model.
        inputs = {
            key: value.to(self.model.device)
            if hasattr(value, "to")
            else value
            for key, value in inputs.items()
        }

        with torch.inference_mode():
            generated_ids = self.model.generate(
                **inputs,
                max_new_tokens=self.cfg.get(
                    "max_new_tokens",
                    512,
                ),
                do_sample=False,
            )

        input_length = inputs["input_ids"].shape[1]

        generated_ids = generated_ids[
            :, input_length:
        ]

        text = self.processor.batch_decode(
            generated_ids,
            skip_special_tokens=True,
            clean_up_tokenization_spaces=False,
        )[0]

        return text.strip()


def transcribe(
    regions: list[Region],
    cfg: dict,
) -> list[Chunk]:
    """Convert layout regions into OCR text chunks.
    Checkpoints incrementally to OCR_DIR/chunks.json and resumes from it
    on restart (skips regions whose chunk_id was already OCR'd) -- Kaggle
    sessions can die mid-run, this avoids losing completed work."""

    OCR_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    output_path = OCR_DIR / "chunks.json"
    checkpoint_every = cfg["ocr"].get("checkpoint_every", 25)

    # Resume: load any chunks already OCR'd from a previous run.
    chunks: list[Chunk] = []
    already_done_ids: set[str] = set()
    if output_path.exists():
        with output_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            existing = json.load(f)
        chunks = [Chunk(**item) for item in existing]
        already_done_ids = {c.id for c in chunks}
        print(f"Resuming: {len(chunks)} chunks already OCR'd, skipping those.")

    reader = Reader(cfg)

    for index, region in enumerate(regions):
        doc_id = region.page_id.rsplit("_p", 1)[0]
        chunk_id = f"{region.page_id}_region_{index:04d}"

        if chunk_id in already_done_ids:
            continue  # already OCR'd in a previous (interrupted) run

        try:
            text = reader.transcribe_region(region)
        except Exception as exc:
            print(f"WARNING: OCR failed for region {index}/{len(regions)} ({chunk_id}): {exc}")
            continue

        if not text:
            continue

        chunks.append(Chunk(
            id=chunk_id, doc_id=doc_id, text=text,
            page_ids=[region.page_id], score=0.0,
        ))

        if (index + 1) % checkpoint_every == 0 or (index + 1) == len(regions):
            with output_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
                json.dump([c.model_dump() for c in chunks], f, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)
            print(f"[{index + 1}/{len(regions)}] checkpoint saved -- {len(chunks)} chunks total")

    return chunksdebashmita@debashmita-HP:~/Documents/4-1/CSE 429 - Deep Learning/doc-agent-G21$ 
    the ocr code is running in kaggle by 3 of our teammates, we divided the issues among us

## Designing `ocr_f1()`

**Why word-level F1, specifically:** "F1" here almost certainly means treating each transcription as a **bag of words**, then computing precision/recall/F1 between predicted and gold word sets — this is the standard, simple approach for OCR quality scoring when you don't need character-level granularity (CER) or strict sequence alignment (WER with edit distance). Given the function is just named `ocr_f1` with no extra parameters, this simpler word-overlap approach is the reasonable default.

```python
def ocr_f1(pred: str, gold: str) -> float:
    """Word-level F1 between OCR output and ground truth."""
    pred_words = pred.lower().split()
    gold_words = gold.lower().split()

    if not pred_words and not gold_words:
        return 1.0
    if not pred_words or not gold_words:
        return 0.0

    from collections import Counter
    pred_counts = Counter(pred_words)
    gold_counts = Counter(gold_words)

    overlap = sum((pred_counts & gold_counts).values())  # multiset intersection

    precision = overlap / len(pred_words)
    recall = overlap / len(gold_words)

    if precision + recall == 0:
        return 0.0

    return 2 * precision * recall / (precision + recall)
```

**Why each design choice:**
- `.lower()` — case normalization, since OCR models often get capitalization slightly wrong (e.g., "The" vs "the") even when the actual word is correctly read; penalizing that would understate real quality
- `Counter` + multiset intersection (`&`) — properly handles repeated words (e.g., if gold has "the" 5 times and pred has "the" 3 times, overlap counts only 3, not treating every "the" as a full match) — more accurate than naive set intersection
- Edge cases (`both empty → 1.0`, `one empty → 0.0`) — avoids division-by-zero crashes on blank pages, which you specifically found exist in your corpus (Cell 5 of `eda.ipynb`)

AI: That confirms it — the file is **truncated**, not corrupted with a stray byte. Look at what your earlier `sed` output showed at line 29352:
```
  {
    "id
```
The entry just... stops, mid-field, no closing quote, no value, nothing after. This means the Kaggle session almost certainly died mid-write while generating this file (or mid-download/transfer) — the JSON array was never properly closed with a final `]`.

This is actually a known risk already flagged in `ocr.py`'s own docstring — `transcribe()`'s checkpointing exists specifically because *"Kaggle sessions can die mid-run"* — but that checkpointing writes a valid file every `checkpoint_every` steps. This particular file is probably either a raw mid-session snapshot copied out before a clean checkpoint, or something got cut short during transfer to your machine.

**Fix: recover every complete object up to the truncation point, discard the broken tail, close the array properly.** This is safe and standard — we're not guessing at missing data, just keeping everything that's genuinely complete:

```bash
python3 << 'EOF'
import json

def recover_json_array(path):
    with open(path, 'r', encoding='utf-8', errors='replace') as f:
        text = f.read().strip()

    assert text.startswith('['), "Expected a JSON array"

    decoder = json.JSONDecoder()
    idx = 1  # skip the opening [
    n = len(text)
    items = []

    while idx < n:
        while idx < n and text[idx] in ' \t\n\r,':
            idx += 1
        if idx >= n or text[idx] == ']':
            break
        try:
            obj, end = decoder.raw_decode(text, idx)
        except json.JSONDecodeError:
            break  # first incomplete/broken object -- stop here
        items.append(obj)
        idx = end

    return items

for path in ['/home/debashmita/Documents/83-12.json', '/home/debashmita/Documents/81-05.json']:
    items = recover_json_array(path)
    print(f"{path}: recovered {len(items)} complete chunks")
    out_path = path.replace('.json', '_recovered.json')
    with open(out_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        json.dump(items, f, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)
    print(f"  -> saved to {out_path}")
EOF
```

This walks the array object-by-object using `raw_decode`, keeps every fully-valid chunk, and stops cleanly the moment it hits the broken one at the end — nothing silently corrupted, nothing guessed.

Run it and paste the output — it'll tell us exactly how many chunks survived from each file (likely thousands, given the truncation point was near char 1.26M into what's probably a multi-MB file). Once we have `_recovered.json` versions for both, we move to Step 2 (matching against your `labels.jsonl` page_ids).

**Step 3 — write the actual scoring script.** This needs to: (1) load `labels.jsonl` ground truth, (2) load both recovered OCR files, (3) for each target page, pull all its chunks, **sort by region number** (important — chunks need to be joined in reading order, not whatever order they happen to sit in the file) and concatenate their text, (4) run `ocr_f1()` against the matching ground truth.

```bash
python3 << 'EOF'
import json, re, sys
sys.path.insert(0, 'src')
from doc_agent.eval.metrics import ocr_f1

# --- Load ground truth ---
gold = {}
with open('grading_kit/labels.jsonl') as f:
    for line in f:
        d = json.loads(line)
        gold[d['page_id']] = d['text']

# --- Load OCR output (both recovered files) ---
chunks = []
for path in ['data/interim/ocr/83-12_recovered.json', 'data/interim/ocr/81-05_recovered.json']:
    with open(path) as f:
        chunks.extend(json.load(f))

print(f"Total gold entries: {len(gold)}")
print(f"Total OCR chunks loaded: {len(chunks)}")
print()

# --- Map: normalized page_id (hyphenated, unpadded) -> OCR page_id (underscored, padded) ---
def normalize(ocr_page_id):
    # e.g. "1983_12_BYTE_08-12_Easy_Software_p0019" -> "byte-magazine-1983-12_p019"
    m = re.match(r"(\d{4})_(\d{2})_BYTE.*_p0*(\d+)$", ocr_page_id)
    if not m:
        return None
    year, month, page = m.groups()
    return f"byte-magazine-{year}-{month}_p{int(page):03d}"

# --- Group chunks by normalized page_id, sorted by region order ---
def region_num(chunk_id):
    m = re.search(r"_region_(\d+)$", chunk_id)
    return int(m.group(1)) if m else 0

pages = {}
for c in chunks:
    for pid in c['page_ids']:
        norm = normalize(pid)
        if norm:
            pages.setdefault(norm, []).append(c)

# --- Score each page that exists in BOTH gold and OCR output ---
for page_id, gold_text in gold.items():
    if page_id in pages:
        page_chunks = sorted(pages[page_id], key=lambda c: region_num(c['id']))
        pred_text = " ".join(c['text'] for c in page_chunks)
        score = ocr_f1(pred_text, gold_text)
        print(f"{page_id}: F1 = {score:.3f}  ({len(page_chunks)} chunks, {len(pred_text.split())} pred words vs {len(gold_text.split())} gold words)")
EOF
```

Run this — it should print real F1 scores for `1983-12_p019` and `1981-05_p132` (the only two pages your current data covers). This is your first genuine, end-to-end Section 5 evidence: real OCR output from your actual pipeline scored against real hand-transcribed ground truth.

AI: This changes everything — it's not an OCR quality failure at all. **The OCR output for `p0019` is a SuperCalc3 spreadsheet advertisement.** Your gold text for the same `page_id` is a "Letters" section discussing FORTH and a book review. These are two completely different pages of the magazine. The low F1 score isn't measuring bad OCR — it's measuring a **page-alignment mismatch**: whatever page ended up labeled `p0019` in the OCR pipeline is not the same physical page that got hand-transcribed as `byte-magazine-1983-12_p019` in `labels.jsonl`.

This is actually a more important finding than a genuine OCR failure would have been — it points to a real bug in how page numbers get assigned somewhere in the pipeline (ingest → layout → OCR chain), not a model-quality issue.

**Step 1 — figure out where the misalignment comes from.** A common cause: **off-by-one indexing** — if `ingest/loader.py` numbers pages 0-indexed internally (page 0 = magazine's actual page 1) but the hand-transcription in `labels.jsonl` was done by looking at a PDF viewer's 1-indexed page count, everything downstream would be shifted by exactly one.

Check nearby pages in the OCR output to see if the "Letters/FORTH" content shows up under a *different* page number:

```bash

**Why this matters more than the F1 score itself:** if this offset is systematic (applies to every page in `1983-12`, not just this one), then **every page_id in `labels.jsonl` for this issue is silently pointing at the wrong OCR output**, which would badly understate your real OCR quality — you'd be comparing correct OCR text against the *wrong* gold answer, not measuring actual transcription errors.

## 2. Step 1 — Structure in the Page Image

**The underlying CV theory, plainly:**

A vision model exploiting page structure relies on two related properties of images:

1. **Local pixel continuity** — a character's strokes are formed by *nearby* pixels sharing similar intensity/color. A convolutional model (or a modern detector like YOLO) builds up "character" and "line" concepts from small local patterns before combining them into larger structures (words, columns, blocks). This is why layout/OCR models work on raw pixels at all — the information needed to recognize a stroke is spatially local, not scattered randomly across the page.

2. **Translation invariance** — a letter "e" looks like an "e" whether it appears at the top-left or bottom-right of the page. This is what lets a single trained model detect text regions anywhere on a page without needing to be retrained per position — it's the property that makes region *detection* (as opposed to hardcoded fixed zones) even feasible.

**How this specifically justifies your pipeline's choices:**

Your data speciality is **multi-column reading order** — and this is precisely a *page-level structural regularity*, not a text-level one. A vision model can detect "this rectangular region is a column of body text, that one is a heading, that one is a figure" purely from pixel layout, before any character is even read. This is exactly why `layout.py` runs **before** `ocr.py` in your pipeline: layout detection exploits page-image structure (regions, boundaries, column gaps) to produce `Region` objects, and OCR only afterward reads pixels *within* each already-correctly-ordered region.

Your own `eda.ipynb` evidence backs this directly — Cell 3 visually confirmed genuine multi-column layout on real pages, and Cell 4 showed page dimensions are consistent-but-not-uniform (whole-issue shifts, occasional inserts), which matters because a layout model needs to handle real geometric variation, not assume one fixed page template.

    10	_LABEL_MAP = {
    11	    "title": "heading",
    12	    "text": "text",
    13	    "plain text": "text",
    14	    "list": "text",
    15	    "table": "table",
    16	    "figure": "figure",

    25	    global _model
    26	
    27	    if _model is not None:
    28	        return _model

    44	
    45	def _sort_reading_order(
    46	    boxes: list[dict],
    47	    page_width: int,
    48	) -> list[dict]:
    49	    """Sort regions approximately in multi-column reading order."""
    50	
    51	    if not boxes:
    52	        return boxes
    53	
    54	    gap_px = 0.04 * page_width
    55	
    56	    columns: list[list[dict]] = []
    57	
    58	    for box in sorted(
    59	        boxes,
    60	        key=lambda b: (b["bbox"][0], b["bbox"][1]),
    61	    ):
    62	        x0 = box["bbox"][0]
    63	        placed = False
    64	
    65	        for column in columns:
    66	            column_x0 = min(
    67	                b["bbox"][0] for b in column
    68	            )
    69	
    70	            if abs(x0 - column_x0) <= gap_px * 3:
    71	                column.append(box)
    72	                placed = True
    73	                break
    74	
    75	        if not placed:
    76	            columns.append([box])
    77	
    78	    columns.sort(
    79	        key=lambda column: min(
    80	            b["bbox"][0] for b in column
    81	        )
    82	    )
    83	
    84	    ordered: list[dict] = []
    85	
    86	    for column in columns:
    87	        column.sort(
    88	            key=lambda b: b["bbox"][1]
    89	        )
    90	        ordered.extend(column)
    91	
    92	    return ordered
    93	
    94	
    95	def _save_regions(
    96	    regions: list[Region],
    97	    doc_id: str,
    98	) -> None:
    99	    """Save detected regions as JSON for reuse by later pipeline stages."""
   100	
   101	    REGION_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
   102	
   103	    output_path = REGION_DIR / f"{doc_id}.json"
   104	
   105	    with output_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
   106	        json.dump(
   107	            [region.model_dump() for region in regions],
   108	            f,
   109	            indent=2,
   110	        )
   111	    print(f"Saved {len(regions)} regions to {output_path}")
   112

   114	def detect(
   115	    pages: list[Page],
   116	    cfg: dict,
   117	) -> list[Region]:
   118	    """Detect layout regions and save them for later pipeline stages."""
   119	
   120	    if not pages:
   121	        return []
   122	
   123	    model = _get_model(cfg)
   124	
   125	    score_thr = (
   126	        cfg.get("layout", {})
   127	        .get("score_thr")
   128	    )
   129	
   130	    device = cfg.get("device", "cuda")
   131	    regions: list[Region] = []
   132	    for page in pages:
   133	        image_path = Path(page.image_path)
   134	
   135	        with Image.open(image_path) as image:
   136	            image = image.convert("RGB")
   137	
   138	            results = model.predict(
   139	                image,
   140	                conf=score_thr,
   141	                device=device,
   142	                verbose=False,
   143	            )
   144	
   145	            boxes: list[dict] = []
   146	
   147	            for result in results:
   148	                if result.boxes is None:
   149	                    continue
   150	
   151	                for box in result.boxes:
   152	                    x0, y0, x1, y1 = map(
   153	                        int,
   154	                        box.xyxy[0].tolist(),
   155	                    )
   156	
   157	                    confidence = float(
   158	                        box.conf[0].item()
   159	                    )
   160	
   161	                    class_id = int(
   162	                        box.cls[0].item()
   163	                    )
   164	
   165	                    label = result.names[class_id]
   166	
   167	                    kind = _LABEL_MAP.get(
   168	                        label.lower(),
   169	                        "text",
   170	                    )
   171	
   172	                    boxes.append(
   173	                        {
   174	                            "bbox": (x0, y0, x1, y1),
   175	                            "kind": kind,
   176	                            "score": confidence,
   177	                        }
   178	                    )
   179	
   180	            ordered_boxes = _sort_reading_order(
   181	                boxes,
   182	                page_width=image.width,
   183	            )
   184	
   185	            for box in ordered_boxes:
   186	                regions.append(
   187	                    Region(
   188	                        page_id=page.id,
   189	                        bbox=box["bbox"],
   190	                        kind=box["kind"],
   191	                    )
   192	                )
   193	
   194	    # Save all regions belonging to this document.
   195	    _save_regions(
   196	        regions,
   197	        pages[0].doc_id,
   198	    )
   199	    
   200	    return regions

## Question 2 — confirmed, with real detail for the form

Line 10–17 is exactly the answer: `_LABEL_MAP` translates DocLayout-YOLO's raw output classes (`title`, `text`, `plain text`, `list`, `table`, `figure`) into your fixed `Region.kind` contract (`heading | text | table | figure`). So yes — the model does genuine semantic classification, not just "find a box." And there's a second layer of structure exploitation happening that's *even more directly relevant to your data speciality*: `_sort_reading_order()` (lines 45–92) explicitly groups detected boxes into columns by x-coordinate proximity (`gap_px = 0.04 * page_width`), then orders top-to-bottom *within* each column before moving to the next column left-to-right. **This is your multi-column data speciality being operationalized directly in code** — this function exists specifically because naive top-to-bottom reading would interleave two columns' text incorrectly, exactly the consequence you already wrote into `eda.ipynb`'s Cell 3 markdown.

## Question 3 — let's get a real example

import sys, yaml

2. **`device: cuda`** — your laptop has no GPU (confirmed earlier), so it needs overriding to `cpu` as shown above, and `doclayout_yolo`'s YOLOv10 needs to actually support CPU inference (usually fine, just slower).

    "DocLayout-YOLO-DocStructBench/snapshots/"

**Why copy rather than just use `hf_hub_download`'s own path directly:** `layout.py`'s `_get_model()` has that exact path hardcoded (line 30–35) rather than calling `hf_hub_download` itself — so unless we place the file at that literal path, `detect()` will raise `FileNotFoundError` on line 37 regardless of what else exists on disk. (Worth flagging to Sunanda separately — a hardcoded absolute path tied to one specific snapshot hash is fragile; if HF ever changes that snapshot hash, this breaks for everyone. ## Now let's draft Section 2, using everything we've confirmed

**Structure in the page image:**
> Our layout stage exploits translation-invariant pixel structure via DocLayout-YOLO (DocStructBench checkpoint): nearby pixels form consistent stroke/character patterns regardless of position on the page, letting the model detect and classify regions (`heading`, `text`, `table`, `figure`) anywhere on a page without needing position-specific training. Concretely, on our held-out page `byte-magazine-1988-05_p110`, the model detected 38 regions cleanly separated into two spatial clusters (x≈52-566 and x≈599-1088) — this bounding-box geometry directly reflects our corpus's two-column layout, which is our declared data speciality. Because BYTE's pages are not uniformly sized (Section 3 of A2 found ~7% of pages deviate >50pt from the corpus median), the model must rely on relative, translation-invariant pixel patterns rather than fixed page coordinates.

**Structure in the text:**
> Once regions are detected, `_sort_reading_order()` groups them into columns by x-coordinate proximity, then orders each column top-to-bottom before moving to the next column — this exploits the fact that reading order (not raw top-to-bottom scan order) carries the actual semantic sequence. Our OCR stage (Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct) then reads each region independently using kind-specific prompts (e.g. distinct instructions for `heading` vs. `table` vs. `figure` regions), relying on local sequence/n-gram context within each correctly-ordered region rather than across incorrectly-merged columns.

## Drafting the third box now, with this real evidence

**What should NOT change the answer:**
> Page dimensions are not uniform across our corpus — our own exploration (Section 3) found ~7% of pages deviate more than 50pt from the corpus median, due to both whole-issue scan-setting differences (e.g. `1978-07` entirely) and individual oversized inserts (foldout ads, diagrams) within otherwise-normal issues. Our layout model must produce correct region detection and reading order regardless of this variation, since it relies on relative, translation-invariant pixel patterns rather than fixed page coordinates. We deliberately do not implement an `enhance_page` (deskew/denoise) stage — `enhance: {enabled: false}` in `configs/config.yaml` — trading potential robustness to faded/skewed scans for lower pipeline latency, consistent with our primary NFR (scalable, p95 ≤1.5s/query). We rely instead on DocLayout-YOLO's and Qwen2-VL's pretrained robustness to moderate scan noise, without a dedicated correction stage.

- Reproduced?: **No** (rule-based code, not a pretrained model)

"""Stage 4 — embed chunks"""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np
from ..contracts import Chunk

EMBED_DIR = Path("data/interim/embed")
_model = None


def _get_model(cfg: dict):
    global _model
    if _model is not None:
        return _model
    from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
    model_name = cfg.get("embed", {}).get("model", "jinaai/jina-embeddings-v5-text-small")
    _model = SentenceTransformer(model_name, trust_remote_code=True)
    return _model


def encode(chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> np.ndarray:
    """Embed with cfg['embed']['model']."""
    model = _get_model(cfg)
    texts = [c.text for c in chunks]

    vectors = model.encode(
        texts,
        batch_size=32,
        show_progress_bar=True,
        convert_to_numpy=True,
    )

    expected_dim = cfg.get("embed", {}).get("dim")
    if expected_dim and vectors.shape[1] != expected_dim:
        print(f"Warning: got dim {vectors.shape[1]}, config expected {expected_dim}")

    EMBED_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    np.save(EMBED_DIR / "vectors.npy", vectors)
    with open(EMBED_DIR / "chunk_ids.json", "w") as f:
        json.dump([c.id for c in chunks], f)
    print(f"Saved {vectors.shape[0]} vectors (dim={vectors.shape[1]}) to {EMBED_DIR}")

    return vectors"""Stage 4 — chunk text"""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
from ..contracts import Chunk

from pathlib import Path

from pathlib import Path


def _find_project_root() -> Path:
    current = Path(__file__).resolve()

    for parent in current.parents:
        if (
            (parent / "src").is_dir()
            and (parent / "data").is_dir()
        ):
            return parent

    raise RuntimeError(
        "Could not find project root containing both 'src' and 'data'."
    )

PROJECT_ROOT = _find_project_root()
CHUNK_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "data" / "interim" / "chunks"

def split(chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> list[Chunk]:
    """Re-chunk raw per-region OCR chunks to cfg['index'] size/overlap.
    Input: small, region-sized Chunks from ocr.transcribe() (one per detected
    region — text/table/heading blocks), already in correct multi-column
    reading order from layout.py. This function regroups them, never reorders.
    """
    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})
    target_tokens = index_cfg.get("chunk_tokens", 256)
    overlap_tokens = index_cfg.get("overlap", 32)

    by_doc: dict[str, list[Chunk]] = {}
    for c in chunks:
        by_doc.setdefault(c.doc_id, []).append(c)

    output: list[Chunk] = []

    for doc_id, doc_chunks in by_doc.items():
        buffer_words: list[str] = []
        buffer_page_ids: list[str] = []
        chunk_idx = 0

        def flush():
            nonlocal chunk_idx
            if not buffer_words:
                return
            text = " ".join(buffer_words)
            seen = set()
            ordered_pages = [p for p in buffer_page_ids if not (p in seen or seen.add(p))]
            output.append(Chunk(
                id=f"{doc_id}_c{chunk_idx:04d}",
                doc_id=doc_id,
                text=text,
                page_ids=ordered_pages,
            ))
            chunk_idx += 1

        for c in doc_chunks:
            words = c.text.split()
            for w in words:
                buffer_words.append(w)
                if c.page_ids:
                    buffer_page_ids.extend(c.page_ids)

                if len(buffer_words) >= target_tokens:
                    flush()
                    carry = buffer_words[-overlap_tokens:] if overlap_tokens > 0 else []
                    carry_pages = buffer_page_ids[-1:] if buffer_page_ids else []
                    buffer_words = list(carry)
                    buffer_page_ids = list(carry_pages)

        flush()

    # Checkpoint, matching layout.py/ocr.py's pattern — lets embed.py (or you,
    # re-running this notebook later) reload without recomputing.
    CHUNK_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    doc_ids = {c.doc_id for c in chunks}

    if len(doc_ids) == 1:
        doc_id = next(iter(doc_ids))
        output_name = f"{doc_id}.json"
    else:
        output_name = "chks.json"

    out_path = CHUNK_DIR / output_name
    with out_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump([c.model_dump() for c in output], f, indent=2, ensure_ascii=False)
    print(f"Saved {len(output)} final chunks to {out_path}")

    return outputdebashmita@debashmita-HP:~/Documents/4-1/CSE 429 - Deep Learning/doc-agent-G21$

Me: Development: Tested the knowledge-base stages incrementally: first layout detection and reading order on individual BYTE pages, then OCR outputs, followed by chunking, embedding, and FAISS indexing on a small subset before processing all valid OCR files on Kaggle. Fits the large corpus because full-corpus experimentation is computationally expensive. Small tests make it possible to catch issues such as incorrect project paths, malformed OCR JSON, embedding dimension mismatches, missing FAISS dependencies, and duplicate issue insertion before processing the complete dataset. The cost is that small samples may not expose every failure mode present across the full corpus.



Deployment: FAISS HNSW index, persisted as index.faiss with chunks_meta.json; the index is loaded using store.load() for retrieval. The generated vector database is treated as a data artifact rather than source code and is not committed to Git.

Fits the large corpus because the vector index can be generated from the OCR/chunk/embedding pipeline and does not need to be stored in the source repository. Incremental updates allow newly OCR'd issues to be added without rebuilding the existing index. The cost is that the index must be regenerated or transferred when deploying to a new environment. A dedicated artifact/data-versioning system would become useful for production-scale deployment.



MLOps: data/versioning.py — SHA256-hashes the corpus directory, records a version id + file count + timestamp per snapshot

Fits reproducibility requirements ("which corpus version produced this result"); costs a small amount of hashing time per snapshot; would change to a real tool (DVC) if the team needed branching/rollback across many corpus versions, not just current-state tracking

using this, now prepare the whole design_choices.md file

## On approximating the index/knowledge-base numbers — don't do this

Section 4 of A2 explicitly asks for "concrete numbers proving the index is real" and the walkthrough warns: *"if you claim OCR or retrieval numbers far above what the grader measures on your own files, that gap is flagged as possible result-fabrication."* Making up a chunk count or index size you haven't actually produced is precisely this risk — and unlike a rough estimate, this is a **checkable claim**: the grader can open your actual `index.faiss`/`chunks_meta.json` and see the real numbers directly, so a mismatch isn't subtle, it's immediately visible.

**The honest, safe alternative — report your real partial progress, labeled as partial:**
> "As of submission, 11/15 issues have completed OCR; the index built from these contains **[real chunk count]** chunks at **384-dim** (BGE-small-en-v1.5), stored via FAISS HNSW. The remaining 4 issues are in progress; final numbers will be higher once OCR completes."

This is completely legitimate — Section 6 of A2 literally asks for "the plan for A3," so an honest "here's where we are, here's what's left" is expected, not penalized. Run whatever you actually have right now through `store.py`/`chunk.py` and report **that real number**, however small — don't round up or invent a number for issues that haven't been processed yet.

Me: cat src/doc_agent/index/store.py
"""Stage 4 — vector store"""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np
import faiss
from ..contracts import Chunk

INDEX_DIR = Path("data/index")


def build(chunks: list[Chunk], vectors: np.ndarray, cfg: dict) -> None:
    """Incrementally add new vectors to the existing HNSW index.

    New issues are appended to the existing FAISS index instead of rebuilding
    the entire vector database.

    Safety checks:
    1. Number of chunks must equal number of vectors.
    2. A document/issue (doc_id) must not already exist in the index.
       This prevents accidental duplicate indexing if the same issue is
       processed twice.
    """

    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})

    INDEX_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    index_path = INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"
    meta_path = INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json"

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 1. Basic chunk/vector consistency check
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # FAISS assigns vector IDs based on their position:
    #
    #   FAISS ID 0 -> chunks_meta[0]
    #   FAISS ID 1 -> chunks_meta[1]
    #   ...
    #
    # Therefore these two lists MUST have the same length.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    if len(chunks) != len(vectors):
        raise ValueError(
            f"Chunk/vector mismatch: "
            f"{len(chunks)} chunks but {len(vectors)} vectors."
        )

    if len(chunks) == 0:
        print("No chunks supplied. Nothing to add.")
        return

    vectors = np.ascontiguousarray(vectors.astype("float32"))
    dim = vectors.shape[1]

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 2. Load existing metadata and detect duplicate issues
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # chunks_meta.json contains the metadata for every vector
    # currently stored in FAISS.
    #
    # We use doc_id to determine whether an entire issue has
    # already been indexed.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    if meta_path.exists():
        with meta_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            existing_metadata = json.load(f)
    else:
        existing_metadata = []

    existing_doc_ids = {
        item["doc_id"]
        for item in existing_metadata
    }

    new_doc_ids = {
        c.doc_id
        for c in chunks
    }

    duplicate_doc_ids = new_doc_ids & existing_doc_ids

    if duplicate_doc_ids:
        raise ValueError(
            "\n❌ Duplicate issue detected!\n"
            f"The following doc_id(s) are already indexed:\n"
            + "\n".join(
                f"  - {doc_id}"
                for doc_id in sorted(duplicate_doc_ids)
            )
            + "\n\nRefusing to modify the FAISS index."
            "\nThis prevents accidental duplicate indexing."
        )

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 3. Load existing FAISS index OR create a new one
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    m = index_cfg.get("hnsw_m", 32)
    ef_construction = index_cfg.get("ef_construction", 200)
    ef_search = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

    if index_path.exists():

        print(f"Loading existing FAISS index from {index_path}")

        index = faiss.read_index(str(index_path))

        # The embedding model must produce the same dimensionality
        # as the vectors already stored in the index.
        if index.d != dim:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Embedding dimension mismatch: "
                f"existing index has dim={index.d}, "
                f"but new vectors have dim={dim}."
            )

        print(
            f"Existing index contains {index.ntotal} vectors."
        )
        print(
            f"Adding {len(vectors)} vectors from "
            f"{len(new_doc_ids)} new issue(s)."
        )

    else:

        print(
            "No existing FAISS index found. "
            "Creating a new HNSW index."
        )

        index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(
            dim,
            m,
            faiss.METRIC_INNER_PRODUCT,
        )

        # Controls how thoroughly HNSW searches for good neighbors
        # while constructing the graph.
        index.hnsw.efConstruction = ef_construction

    # efSearch controls search effort at query time.
    index.hnsw.efSearch = ef_search

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 4. Add ONLY the new vectors
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # Existing vectors remain untouched.
    #
    # Example:
    #
    # First run:
    #   FAISS = Issue A
    #
    # Second run:
    #   FAISS = Issue A + Issue B
    #
    # Third run:
    #   FAISS = Issue A + Issue B + Issue C
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    index.add(vectors)

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 5. Save the updated FAISS index
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    faiss.write_index(index, str(index_path))

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 6. Append metadata for the newly indexed chunks
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # The order here MUST match the order in which vectors were
    # added to FAISS.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    existing_metadata.extend(
        c.model_dump()
        for c in chunks
    )

    with meta_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(
            existing_metadata,
            f,
            ensure_ascii=False,
            indent=2,
        )

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 7. Report final index status
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    size_mb = index_path.stat().st_size / (1024 ** 2)

    print(
        f"\n✅ Successfully updated HNSW index."
        f"\n   Added chunks : {len(chunks)}"
        f"\n   Total vectors: {index.ntotal}"
        f"\n   Dimensions   : {dim}"
        f"\n   HNSW M       : {m}"
        f"\n   Index size   : {size_mb:.2f} MB"
        f"\n   Index path   : {index_path}"
    )

def load(cfg: dict):
    """Load the persisted index + its chunk metadata."""
    index_path = INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"
    meta_path = INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json"

    if not index_path.exists():
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"No index found at {index_path} — run build() first.")

    index = faiss.read_index(str(index_path))

    # efSearch doesn't get saved with the index — reset it here in case
    # something changed in cfg since build() ran.
    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})
    index.hnsw.efSearch = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

    with open(meta_path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        chunk_dicts = json.load(f)

    chunks = [Chunk(**d) for d in chunk_dicts]

    print(f"Loaded index: {index.ntotal} vectors, dim={index.d}")
    return index, chunksdebashmita@debashmita-HP:~/Documents/4-1/CSE 429 - Deep Learning/doc-agent-G21$

python3 << 'EOF'
import sys, json, yaml
sys.path.insert(0, 'src')
from doc_agent.index import chunk, embed, store
from doc_agent.contracts import Chunk

with open('configs/config.yaml') as f:
    cfg = yaml.safe_load(f)

all_chunks = []
for path in ['data/interim/ocr/81-05_recovered.json', 'data/interim/ocr/83-12_recovered.json']:
    with open(path) as f:
        raw = json.load(f)
    all_chunks.extend(Chunk(**item) for item in raw)

print(f"Loaded {len(all_chunks)} raw OCR region-chunks")

final_chunks = chunk.split(all_chunks, cfg)
print(f"After chunking: {len(final_chunks)} final chunks")

vectors = embed.encode(final_chunks, cfg)
print(f"Embedded: {vectors.shape}")

store.build(final_chunks, vectors, cfg)
EOF
```

## 3. One-line pipeline summary

> `pages → clean (fitz/PyMuPDF render) → (enhance: disabled) → layout (DocLayout-YOLO, region+reading-order detection) → OCR (Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct, region-kind-specific prompts) → chunk (256 words, 32-word overlap, reading order preserved) → embed (BGE-small-en-v1.5, 384-dim) → store (FAISS HNSW, incremental append)`

**Custom tweaks worth calling out specifically:**
- **Enhance is entirely skipped**, not just a no-op default — a deliberate trade against our scalability NFR
- **Chunking never re-sorts** — it trusts layout's reading order completely, by design (`chunk.py`'s own docstring states this explicitly)
- **Indexing is incremental, not batch-rebuild** — `store.py`'s `build()` appends new-issue vectors to an existing index and refuses duplicate `doc_id`s, supporting your issue-by-issue OCR completion plan

Me: """Stage 1 — load scanned page-images."""

from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from pdf2image import convert_from_path

from ..contracts import Page

RAW_DIR = Path("data/raw")

PAGE_DIR = Path("data/interim/pages")

def load_pages(cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:

    """Read data/raw/ and return scanned pages as Page objects."""

    pdf_files = sorted(RAW_DIR.glob("*.pdf"))

    if not pdf_files:

        raise FileNotFoundError(

            f"No PDF files found in {RAW_DIR.resolve()}"

        )

    if len(pdf_files) > 1:

        raise ValueError(

            f"Expected one PDF in {RAW_DIR}, "

            f"but found {len(pdf_files)}. "

            "Process one issue at a time."

        )

    pdf_path = pdf_files[0]

    doc_id = pdf_path.stem

    output_dir = PAGE_DIR / doc_id

    output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    pages: list[Page] = []

    page_number = 1

    while True:

        image_path = output_dir / f"page_{page_number:04d}.png"

        # Reuse an already-converted page if it exists.

        if image_path.exists():

            pages.append(

                Page(

                    id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_number:04d}",

                    image_path=str(image_path),

                    doc_id=doc_id,

                )

            )

            page_number += 1

            continue

        try:

            images = convert_from_path(

                str(pdf_path),

                dpi=150,

                first_page=page_number,

                last_page=page_number,

                fmt="png",

            )

        except Exception as exc:

            if page_number == 1:

                raise RuntimeError(

                    f"Failed to convert the first page of {pdf_path}"

                ) from exc

            break

        # No image means we have reached the end of the PDF.

        if not images:

            break

        images[0].save(image_path, "PNG")

        pages.append(

            Page(

                id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_number:04d}",

                image_path=str(image_path),

                doc_id=doc_id,

            )

        )

        page_number += 1

    return pages

this is loader.py

Since your `data/raw/` currently has all 15 issues sitting in it, this function can't be run as-is against your current corpus folder — it's built to process **one issue at a time**, meaning some orchestration (a loop that stages one PDF at a time, or per-issue Kaggle sessions) is expected around it. This actually lines up with how your team's OCR work has been split — issue-by-issue across teammates — so it's likely intentional design, not a bug, but worth confirming with whoever wrote it that this is the real intended usage pattern rather than an oversight nobody's hit yet.

**One other useful thing this confirms:** pages are 1-indexed starting from the PDF's actual page 1 (`page_number = 1`, `page_0001.png`) — no off-by-one built into the loader itself. Act as our Lead AI Architect. Help me draft a concrete, technically sound plan for Section 6 that prepares our system for Milestone 3 and aligns with all grading criteria.

### Please guide me through completing the three components of Section 6:

1. **What We'll Build Next:**

   - Help me structure our plan for retrieval + reranking, the agent loop + tools (`src/doc_agent/agent/`), guardrails, grounding, and evaluation.

   - **Mandatory Feature:** Explicitly detail how we will implement **evidence-gated re-search** (widening $k$ on weak retrieval confidence, abstaining at $k_{\text{max}}$) to meet the fail-closed requirement.

2. **Our Evaluation Set (`tasks.jsonl` Design):**

   - Provide a strategy for authoring domain-specific query-answer pairs in `grading_kit/tasks.jsonl`[cite: 2].

   - Explain how we will balance checkable/verifiable items with unanswerable (abstention) edge cases to properly test our agent[cite: 2].

3. **Biggest Risk / Unknown & Mitigation:**

   - Identify the primary failure mode or technical risk for our specific domain in A3 (e.g., hallucinated citations, infinite tool loops, or OCR noise propagating to retrieval)[cite: 2].

AI: ## 1. What We'll Build Next

**Retrieval + reranking:** implement `retrieve.py` around the exact config already committed — `k=10` default, `cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L6-v2` reranker, querying the FAISS HNSW index built in A2 (Row 4). Nothing here needs new design decisions; A3's job is to actually wire the already-configured parameters into working code.

**Agent loop + tools (`agent/`):** an LLM controller (model still to be chosen) operating over the 9 fixed tools (`retrieve`, `rerank`, `read_page`, `enhance_page`, `extract`, `aggregate`, `cite`, `calculator`, `escalate_to_human`), bounded by `max_steps=8` and `budget_usd=0.05`/query, with `autonomy: "act-with-approval"` gating higher-stakes actions.

**Evidence-gated re-search — the mandatory fail-closed behavior, concretely:**
1. Call `retrieve` with `k=10`.
2. Check the top result's score against `weak_threshold=0.35`.
3. If below threshold, widen: `k += k_step (10)`, re-retrieve, repeat.
4. If still weak once `k` reaches `k_max=40`, **abstain** — return "not enough evidence" rather than guessing.

This isn't a new design — it's already fully specified in `config.yaml`'s `retrieve` block. A3's task is implementing the loop exactly as configured, plus a unit test proving abstention actually fires at `k_max` on a genuinely out-of-corpus question (not just on paper).

**Guardrails/grounding:** every answer must cite a real page (`cite` tool); prompt-injection defense sanitizes page text so it's never treated as an instruction; grounding attaches immediately before the final answer is returned, not earlier in the loop.

**Evaluation:** implement the remaining `eval/metrics.py` stubs (`recall_at_k`, `groundedness`, `citation_accuracy`, `ece`, `subgroup_gap`) the same way `ocr_f1()` was built — synthetic known-input tests before running against real pipeline output.

## 2. `tasks.jsonl` design strategy

- **Gold answers sourced from `labels.jsonl`** — your 15 hand-transcribed held-out pages already have verified ground truth, so verifiable questions can be authored directly against known facts on those specific pages (dates, names, numbers explicitly stated in the transcribed text).
- **Balance**, per the walkthrough's own example ratio (Team G07: 20 verifiable / 6 judged / 4 out-of-corpus out of 30): aim for roughly **60–70% verifiable** (exact-match, cheap and objective to grade), **20–25% judged** (multi-step reasoning across a page, scored by LLM-judge with a stated rubric), and **10–15% out-of-corpus** (questions about topics BYTE never covered, or specific issues outside your 15 — correct answer is abstention).
- **Out-of-corpus questions specifically should be adversarial-ish**, not obviously irrelevant — e.g. a plausible-sounding question about a real 1980s computing topic that just isn't covered by your specific 15 issues, so abstention is actually being tested, not just triggered by an obviously nonsensical prompt.

## 3. Biggest risk — grounded in what you already found tonight

**OCR/layout noise propagating silently into retrieval and grounding.** This isn't hypothetical — you already have direct evidence of it:
- The `1983-12_p019` page-indexing offset (verified: gold text for "p019" actually matches OCR output labeled "p020") shows page-id mismatches can happen and produce a confidently-wrong-looking low score that's actually a bookkeeping bug, not a real failure.
- The duplicate near-identical bounding boxes seen in your own `layout.py` output (page 110: `heading (673,1442,1011,1467)` appearing twice) suggest occasional double-detection, which could double-count or muddy a chunk's embedded meaning.
- Two of your OCR JSON files arrived **truncated** from Kaggle session death — if this happens on a batch run without you noticing (unlike tonight's careful recovery), silently incomplete text would feed the index without any error.

**Mitigation:** 
1. Add a validation step after chunking that flags any chunk whose source page_id doesn't appear in `labels.jsonl` (for held-out pages) as a sanity check before trusting retrieval scores on those pages.
2. Extend `store.py`'s existing duplicate-`doc_id` detection logic (already built, tested) with duplicate-region detection at the layout stage, using IoU (bounding box overlap) to catch double-detections before they reach OCR.
3. Keep the checkpoint-and-resume pattern (already proven in `ocr.py`) as the standard for every future long-running Kaggle stage, specifically to avoid repeating tonight's truncated-file recovery scramble.

Unverified Assumptions: I rejected any implicit assumptions about our dataset's exact noise characteristics or model choices, insisting that missing parameters be verified against our actual EDA notebook before finalized.
